\displaystyle\leq C\bigg\{\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{2r,+}}|\nabla u^{\varepsilon}|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}+\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{2r,+}}|F^{\varepsilon}|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq C(M+M^{4+2\beta+2\delta}).

On the other hand, let
N\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
2^{N}r\in[{\frac{1}{32},\frac{1}{16}}]
. Then

\displaystyle\bigg|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{r,+}}p^{\varepsilon}-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{1/2,+}}p^{\varepsilon}\bigg|\leq\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\bigg|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{2^{j}r,+}}p^{\varepsilon}-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{2^{j+1}r,+}}p^{\varepsilon}\bigg|+\bigg|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{2^{N}r,+}}p^{\varepsilon}-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{1/2,+}}p^{\varepsilon}\bigg|.

Now, observe that for each
j={0,1,\cdots,N-1}
,

\displaystyle\bigg|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{2^{j}r,+}}p^{\varepsilon}-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{2^{j+1}r,+}}p^{\varepsilon}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq 4\int_{2^{j+3}r}^{2^{j+4}r}\frac{1}{\tilde{r}}\sup_{s,t\in[1/16,1/4]}\bigg|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{s\tilde{r},+}}p^{\varepsilon}-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{t\tilde{r},+}}p^{\varepsilon}\bigg|\,{\rm d}\tilde{r}.

Thus, (
3.40
) leads to

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{{N-1}}\bigg|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{2^{j}r,+}}p^{\varepsilon}-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{2^{j+1}r,+}}p^{\varepsilon}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq 4\int_{\varepsilon}^{1/2}\frac{1}{\tilde{r}}\sup_{s,t\in[1/16,1/4]}\bigg|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{s\tilde{r},+}}p^{\varepsilon}-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{t\tilde{r},+}}p^{\varepsilon}\bigg|\,{\rm d}\tilde{r}

\displaystyle\leq C(M+M^{4+2\beta+2\delta}).

Finally, by the same trick as in (
3.6
), we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \bigg|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{2^{N}r,+}}p^{\varepsilon}-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{1/2,+}}p^{\varepsilon}\bigg|&\leq C\bigg\{\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{1,+}}|\nabla u^{\varepsilon}|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}+\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{1,+}}|F^{\varepsilon}|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\}\\
&\leq C(M+M^{2}).\end{aligned}

Summarizing up the above estimates, we obtain the desired estimate for the pressure
p^{\varepsilon}
.
This completes the proof of Theorem
A
.

\Box

4.
Boundary layers in bumpy John domains

As seen in the previous section, the no-slip Stokes polynomials of degree
1
(i.e., the basis of
\mathscr{P}_{1}
)

P^{(11)}=(x_{3},0,0),\hskip 10.00002ptP^{(12)}=(0,x_{3},0)

(4.1)

are the key ingredients for the large-scale Lipschitz estimate.
Their trace on non-flat bumpy boundaries can be corrected by adding boundary layer correctors. Consequently, one obtains polynomial solutions of the Stokes equations in the bumpy John domains considered in this paper.

In Subsection
4.1
, we determine the no-slip Stokes polynomials of
2
by explicit computation. The boundary layer equations are introduced as well. Subsections
4.2
and
4.3
are respectively devoted to the analysis of the first-order and the second-order boundary layer equations. The estimates for the Green function, obtained in Appendix
B
using the large-scale Lipschitz estimate of Theorem
A
, play a fundamental role. We summarize the estimates for the boundary layers in Subsection
4.4
. These estimates are key to the theory of higher-order regularity in Section
5
.

4.1.
No-slip Stokes polynomials

Let
u
be a solution of
-\Delta u+\nabla p=0
and
\nabla\cdot u=0
in
Q_{1,+}(0)
and
u=0
on
\partial\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}\cap B_{1}(0)
. The real analyticity of
u
in
Q_{1/2,+}(0)
is classical and well-known; see
[
69
,
46
]
. Here we want to identify the form of the no-slip Stokes polynomials of degree
2
of
u
at
0
.

Let
P(x)=(P_{1}(x),P_{2}(x),P_{3}(x))
be the no-slip Stokes polynomials of degree
2
of
u
at
0
. First of all, since
u=0
on
\partial\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}
, then we must have

\displaystyle P_{1}(x)

\displaystyle=a_{1}x_{3}+b_{11}x_{1}x_{3}+b_{12}x_{2}x_{3}+b_{13}x_{3}^{2},

(4.2)

\displaystyle P_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=a_{2}x_{3}+b_{21}x_{1}x_{3}+b_{22}x_{2}x_{3}+b_{23}x_{3}^{2},

\displaystyle P_{3}(x)

\displaystyle=b_{31}x_{1}x_{3}+b_{32}x_{2}x_{3}+b_{33}x_{3}^{2}.

The linear part is familiar. So let us concentrate on the quadratic part. Note that there are no terms
x_{1}^{2},x_{2}^{2}
, or
x_{1}x_{2}
, because
u=0
on the boundary. If there is no further restriction on
u
, then there are
9
free variables
b_{ij},1\leq i,j\leq 3
, as shown in (
4.2
).
If
\nabla\cdot u=0
in
Q_{1/2,+}(0)
, then we claim that
P
is also divergence-free. If this claim is true, then we must have

b_{11}+b_{22}+2b_{33}=0,\hskip 10.00002ptb_{31}=b_{32}=0.

Because of this restriction on the coefficients, the dimension for the homogeneous no-slip Stokes polynomials of degree
2
becomes
6
. We can find basis polynomials as follows:

\displaystyle P^{(21)}=(x_{2}x_{3},0,0),\hskip 10.00002ptP^{(22)}=(x_{3}^{2},0,0),

(4.3)

\displaystyle P^{(23)}=(0,x_{1}x_{3},0),\hskip 10.00002ptP^{(24)}=(0,x_{3}^{2},0),

\displaystyle P^{(25)}=(-2x_{1}x_{3},0,x_{3}^{2}),\hskip 10.00002ptP^{(26)}=(0,-2x_{2}x_{3},x_{3}^{2}).

Note that these polynomials are solutions to the stationary Stokes system with associated pressure
L^{(2j)}
given by

\displaystyle L^{(2j)}(x)=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for }j=1,3.

(4.4)

\displaystyle L^{(22)}(x)=2x_{1},\ L^{(24)}(x)=2x_{2},

\displaystyle\text{and }

\displaystyle L^{2j}(x)=2x_{3},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for }j=5,6.

Now, let us show the claim that
P
is divergence-free. Since
u=P+O(|x|^{3})
, we have that
\nabla\cdot u=\nabla\cdot P+O(|x|^{2})=0
in
\{x_{3}\geq 0\}\cap B_{1/2}(0)
. Because of
\nabla\cdot P=C_{0}+C_{1}\cdot x
for some
C_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
and
C_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we see that
C_{0}+C_{1}\cdot x=O(|x|^{2})
. Hence we must have
C_{0}=0
and
C_{1}=0
; otherwise, it is easy to find a contradiction by taking
x=\delta C_{1}
or
-\delta C_{1}
for sufficiently small
\delta
.

Similarly to the linear solution pairs
(P^{(1j)},0)
, the fundamental fact about the polynomial pairs constructed above is that
(P^{(2j)},L^{(2j)})
are quadratic solutions of Stokes equations in the upper half-space
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}
, namely

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\Delta P^{(2j)}+\nabla L^{(2j)}=0&\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}\\
\nabla\cdot P^{(2j)}=0&\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}\\
P^{(2j)}=0&\text{on }\partial\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}.\end{array}\right.

To study the
C^{1,\gamma}
and
C^{2,\gamma}
regularity of (
NSε
), the linear and quadratic solutions of Stokes equations in
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}
are not enough. We need to construct linear and quadratic solutions in
\Omega
which vanish on
\partial\Omega
, where
\Omega
is a bumpy John half-space in the sense of Definition
1.2
. These solutions will be constructed based on
(P^{(1j)},0)
and
(P^{(2j)},L^{(2j)})
. Observe that
P^{(ij)}
does not vanish on
\partial\Omega
. Therefore we have to introduce new correctors, called boundary layers, in order to correct the boundary discrepancy on
\partial\Omega
. Precisely, we will
show the existence of weak solutions (with corresponding sublinear or subquadratic growth) of the following boundary layer equations

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\Delta v+\nabla q=0&\text{in }\Omega\\
\nabla\cdot v=0&\text{in }\Omega\\
v+P^{(ij)}=0&\text{on }\partial\Omega,\end{array}\right.

(BL
{}_{i{\rm th}}^{(j)}
)

where
i\in\{1,2\}
. Here a couple
(v,q)\in H^{1}_{{\rm loc}}(\overline{\Omega})^{3}\times L^{2}_{{\rm loc}}(\overline{\Omega})
is said to be a weak solution of (
BL

⁢
i
th

(
j
)

) if it satisfies: (i)
\nabla\cdot v=0
in the sense of distributions, (ii)
\chi(v+P^{(ij)})\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)^{3}
for any
\chi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, and (iii) the weak formulation:

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\nabla v\cdot\nabla\phi-\int_{\Omega}q(\nabla\cdot\phi)=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for any }\phi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)^{3}.

(4.5)

4.2.
First-order boundary layers

We consider the first-order boundary layer equations

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\Delta v+\nabla q=0&\text{in }\Omega\\
\nabla\cdot v=0&\text{in }\Omega\\
v+P^{(1j)}=0&\text{on }\partial\Omega,\end{array}\right.

(BL
{}_{{\rm 1st}}^{(j)}
)

for
j\in\{1,2\}
. The solvability of (
BL

⁢
1
s
t

(
j
)

) follows from the next statement.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
L\in(0,\infty)
and
\Omega
be a bumpy John domain with constant
L
according to Definition
1.2
. For
j\in\{1,2\}
, there exists a unique weak solution
(v^{(1j)},q^{(1j)})\in H^{1}_{{\rm loc}}(\overline{\Omega})^{3}\times L^{2}_{{\rm loc}}(\overline{\Omega})
of (
BL

⁢
1
s
t

(
j
)

)
satisfying

\displaystyle\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\int_{\Omega\cap(\xi+(0,1)^{2})\times\mathbb{R}}\big(|\nabla v^{(1j)}|^{2}+|q^{(1j)}|^{2}\big)\leq C,

(4.6)

where the constant
C
depends only on
L
.

In the work
[
53
]
the well-posedness of the system (
BL

⁢
1
s
t

(
j
)

) was proved over Lipschitz graphs by a domain decomposition method: coupling of the Stokes problem in a bumpy channel
\Omega\cap\{x_{3}<0\}
with the Stokes problem in the flat half-space
\{x_{3}>0\}
via a nonlocal Dirichlet to Neumann boundary condition at the interface
\{x_{3}=0\}
. We face considerable technical difficulties when trying to adapt this strategy to the case of bumpy John domains. Indeed, the local energy estimates in the bumpy channel require to estimate the pressure, or to work with divergence-free test functions. In either case, we need to construct a Bogovskii operator for a sequence of exhausting domains containing
\Omega\cap\{|x^{\prime}|\leq k,\,x_{3}<0\}
with a constant uniform in
k
. The construction of the Bogovskii operator of Theorem
A.1
by
[
2
]
relies on connecting any point in the bumpy John domain to a fixed neighborhood of a reference point
\tilde{x}
. Such a procedure gives, for a slim domain such as
\Omega\cap\{|x^{\prime}|\leq k,\,x_{3}<0\}
, a constant in the estimate (
A.1
) that scales proportionately to the horizontal size
k
of the domain. We are unable to take advantage of the small vertical extent of the domain to provide a modified construction of the Bogovskii operator. This would be needed to carry out the downward iteration on the local energy estimates, also called Saint-Venant estimates, in
[
53
]
.

Here we take advantage of the fact that we already proved large-scale Lipschitz estimates by the quantitative method, without relying on boundary layers as in
[
53
]
. Therefore, we develop a new strategy using the large-scale Lipschitz estimate to prove the existence of solutions to (
BL

⁢
1
s
t

(
j
)

). We rely on the Green kernel estimates proved in Appendix
B
. For
N\in\mathbb{R}
, let us set

\Omega_{\leq N}:=\Omega\cap\{z_{3}\leq N\},\hskip 20.00003pt\Omega_{\geq N}:=\Omega\cap\{z_{3}\geq N\}.

(4.7)

We also define
\Omega_{<N}
and
\Omega_{>N}
in a similar manner.

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

We denote
(v^{(1j)},q^{(1j)})
by
(v,q)
, not to burden the notation.

(
Uniqueness
) Let
P^{(1j)}=0
in (
BL

⁢
1
s
t

(
j
)

). Then the Liouville-type result, Corollary
3.1
, implies
v=0
in the class

\displaystyle\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\int_{\Omega\cap(\xi+(0,1)^{2})\times\mathbb{R}}|\nabla v|^{2}<\infty.

This implies
q=0
in the class (
4.6
) as well from the equations.

(
Existence
)

Step 1: lifting the boundary data.
Let
{\eta_{-}(x_{3})}
be a smooth cut-off function such that

\displaystyle\text{$\eta_{-}(t)$ is smooth and non-negative,}

(4.8)

\displaystyle\text{$\eta_{-}(t)={1}$ if $t<3$ and $\eta_{-}(t)=0$ if $t>4$.}

By writing
w=v+{\eta_{-}}P^{(1j)}
, we see that
w
satisfies

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\Delta w+\nabla q=F:=-\Delta({\eta_{-}}P^{(1j)})&\text{in }\Omega\\
\nabla\cdot w=0&\text{in }\Omega\\
w=0&\text{on }\partial\Omega.\end{array}\right.

(4.9)

Notice that
F
is a bounded function supported in a slim channel
S:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}|\ 3\leq x_{3}\leq 4\}
. Thus, the problem is reduced to finding a weak solution of (
4.9
) satisfying

\displaystyle\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\int_{\Omega\cap(\xi+(0,1)^{2})\times\mathbb{R}}\big(|\nabla w|^{2}+|q|^{2}\big)\leq C\|F\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}.

(4.10)

We rely on the representation of
w
and
q
by the Green kernel

w(x)=\int_{\Omega}G(x,y)F(y)\,{\rm d}y,\hskip 20.00003ptq(x)=\int_{\Omega}\Pi(x,y)\cdot F(y)\,{\rm d}y.

Thanks to the properties of the Green function
(G,\Pi)
, it suffices to prove that
\nabla w
and
q
are well-defined and satisfy the estimate (
4.10
).

In the following proof, we take the zero-extension of
(G,\Pi)
as is done in Appendix
B
.

Step 2: estimate on
\Omega_{\geq 8}
.

For any
y\in S
and
x\in\Omega_{\geq 8}
, by Proposition
B.3
(i)

|\nabla_{x}G(x,y)|\leq\frac{C}{|x-y|^{3}}.

Then it follows from the Hölder’s inequality that

\displaystyle|\nabla w(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{S}|\nabla_{x}G(x,y)||F(y)|\,{\rm d}y

\displaystyle\leq\|F\|_{L^{\infty}}\int_{S}\frac{C}{|x-y|^{3}}\,{\rm d}y

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C\|F\|_{L^{\infty}}}{x_{3}}.

A similar computation using Proposition
B.5
(i) gives the same bound for the pressure
q(x)
with
x_{3}\geq 8
. Consequently,

\displaystyle\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\int_{\Omega_{\geq 8}\cap(\xi+(0,1)^{2})\times\mathbb{R}}\big(|\nabla w|^{2}+|q|^{2}\big)

\displaystyle\leq C\|F\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}.

(4.11)

Step 3: estimate on
\Omega_{\leq 8}
.

Fix
\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
arbitrarily. For simplicity, we denote the cubes in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
centered at
(\xi,0)
by
Q_{R}(\xi)=(\xi,0)+(-R,R)^{3}
. We would like to estimate
|\nabla w|
and
|q|
in the cube
Q_{8}(\xi)
. Notice that
\Omega_{\leq 8}\subset\cup_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\times\{0\}}Q_{8}(\xi)
with finite overlaps.

Taking a cube
Q_{40}(\xi)
, we decompose
F
into two parts as
F=F\chi_{Q_{40}(\xi)}+F(1-\chi_{Q_{40}(\xi)})
. Correspondingly, we decompose
(w,q)
in singular and regular parts, namely
(w,q)=(w_{\rm sing},q_{\rm sing})+(w_{\rm reg},q_{\rm reg})
, where

\left\{\begin{aligned} w_{\rm sing}(x)&=\int_{\Omega}G(x,y)F(y)\chi_{Q_{40}(\xi)}(y)\,{\rm d}y,\\
q_{\rm sing}(x)&=\int_{\Omega}\Pi(x,y)\cdot F(y)\chi_{Q_{40}(\xi)}(y)\,{\rm d}y,\end{aligned}\right.

and

\left\{\begin{aligned} w_{\rm reg}(x)&=\int_{\Omega}G(x,y)F(y)(1-\chi_{Q_{40}(\xi)}(y))\,{\rm d}y,\\
q_{\rm reg}(x)&=\int_{\Omega}\Pi(x,y)\cdot F(y)(1-\chi_{Q_{40}(\xi)}(y))\,{\rm d}y.\end{aligned}\right.

To estimate the regular part
(w_{\rm reg},q_{\rm reg})
in
Q_{8}(\xi)
, we use (
B.19
) and (
B.23
) in Proposition
B.3
to obtain

\bigg(\int_{Q_{8}(\xi)}|\nabla_{x}G(x,y)|^{2}\,{\rm d}x\bigg)^{1/2}\leq\frac{C}{|(\xi,0)-y|^{3}},

for any
y\in S\setminus Q_{40}(\xi)
. As a result,

\displaystyle\bigg(\int_{Q_{8}(\xi)}|\nabla w_{\rm reg}|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\|F\|_{L^{\infty}}\int_{S\setminus Q_{40}(\xi)}\bigg(\int_{Q_{8}(\xi)}|\nabla_{x}G(x,y)|^{2}\,{\rm d}x\bigg)^{1/2}\,{\rm d}y

(4.12)

\displaystyle\leq\|F\|_{L^{\infty}}\int_{S\setminus Q_{40}(\xi)}\frac{C}{|(\xi,0)-y|^{3}}\,{\rm d}y

\displaystyle\leq C\|F\|_{L^{\infty}}.

Similarly, by using (
B.38
) and (
B.39
), we can derive the estimate of
q_{\rm reg}
,

\bigg(\int_{Q_{8}(\xi)}|q_{\rm reg}|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\leq C\|F\|_{L^{\infty}}.

(4.13)

Next, we consider the singular part
(w_{\rm sing},q_{\rm sing})
, which actually is a weak solution of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\Delta w_{\rm sing}+\nabla q_{\rm sing}=F\chi_{Q_{40}(\xi)}&\text{in }\Omega\\
\nabla\cdot w_{\rm sing}=0&\text{in }\Omega\\
w_{\rm sing}=0&\text{on }\partial\Omega.\end{array}\right.

Note that the energy relation yields

\|\nabla w_{\rm sing}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\|F\|_{L^{\infty}},

(4.14)

where
C
is independent of
\xi
. This gives the local
L^{2}
boundedness of
w_{\rm sing}
in the channel
\Omega_{\leq 8}
. On the other hand, the argument in Step 2, using (
B.38
), implies that for any
x_{3}\geq 8
,

|q_{\rm sing}(x)|\leq\frac{C\|F\|_{L^{\infty}}}{x_{3}}.

(4.15)

Let
\Omega_{20}(\xi)
be the John domain given by Definition
1.2
satisfying

\Omega\cap Q_{20}(\xi)\subset\Omega_{20}(\xi)\subset\Omega\cap Q_{40}(\xi).

By the Bogovskii lemma and (
4.14
),

\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{20}(\xi)}|q_{\rm sing}-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{20}(\xi)}q_{\rm sing}|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\leq C\|F\|_{L^{\infty}}.

(4.16)

On the other hand, let
Q_{1}^{*}(\xi)=(\xi,10)+(-1,1)^{3}
. By (
4.15
),

\bigg|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{{Q_{1}^{*}(\xi)}}q_{\rm sing}\bigg|\leq C\|F\|_{L^{\infty}}.

(4.17)

Since
{Q_{1}^{*}(\xi)}\subset\Omega_{20}(\xi)
, by a familiar argument and (
4.16
), we have

\displaystyle\bigg|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q_{1}^{*}({\xi})}q_{\rm sing}-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{20}(\xi)}q_{\rm sing}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q_{1}^{*}({\xi})}|q_{\rm sing}-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{20}(\xi)}q_{\rm sing}|

(4.18)

\displaystyle\leq C\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{20}(\xi)}|q_{\rm sing}-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{20}(\xi)}q_{\rm sing}|\leq C\|F\|_{L^{\infty}}.

This, together with (
4.17
), implies

\bigg|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{20}(\xi)}q_{\rm sing}\bigg|\leq C\|F\|_{L^{\infty}}.

(4.19)

Now, combining (
4.16
) and (
4.19
), we obtain

\displaystyle\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega\cap Q_{8}(\xi)}|q_{\rm sing}|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega\cap Q_{8}(\xi)}|q_{\rm sing}-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{20}(\xi)}q_{\rm sing}|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}+\bigg|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{20}(\xi)}q_{\rm sing}\bigg|

(4.20)

\displaystyle\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{20}(\xi)}|q_{\rm sing}-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{20}(\xi)}q_{\rm sing}|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}+\bigg|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{20}(\xi)}q_{\rm sing}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq C\|F\|_{L^{\infty}}

with
C
independent of
\xi
.

Now, combining (
4.12
), (
4.13
), (
4.14
) and (
4.20
), we have

\displaystyle\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\int_{\Omega\cap Q_{8}(\xi)}\big(|\nabla w|^{2}+|q|^{2}\big)

\displaystyle\leq C\|F\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}.

(4.21)

Finally, the desired estimate (
4.10
) is a consequence of (
4.11
) and (
4.21
). This concludes the proof of Theorem
4.1
.
∎

4.3.
Second-order periodic boundary layers

Let
P^{(2j)}
be a no-slip Stokes polynomial of degree
2
. We consider the second-order boundary layer equations

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\Delta v+\nabla q=0,&x\in\Omega\\
\nabla\cdot v=0,&x\in\Omega\\
v+P^{(2j)}=0,&x\in\partial\Omega.\end{array}\right.

(BL
{}_{{\rm 2nd}}^{(j)}
)

Constructing solutions to (
BL

⁢
2
n
d

(
j
)

) for
j\in\{1,3,5,6\}
with subquadratic growth is much more involved than constructing solutions to (
BL

⁢
1
s
t

(
j
)

) with sublinear growth. Indeed, for
j\in\{1,3,5,6\}
, the boundary data
-P^{(2j)}
in (
BL

⁢
2
n
d

(
j
)

) grows linearly in the tangential direction. Solutions to (
BL

⁢
2
n
d

(
j
)

) for
j\in\{1,3,5,6\}
are constructed using the first-order correctors solving (
BL

⁢
1
s
t

(
j
)

), see below. For this construction we rely on convergence/decay properties of the first-order correctors away from the boundary. Hence, we analyze (
BL

⁢
2
n
d

(
j
)

) under periodicity assumptions. Periodicity ensures exponential convergence/decay away from the boundary.

Throughout this subsection, we assume
\Omega
is a periodic bumpy John domain according to Definition
1.3
. Consider the fundamental periodic domain

\Omega_{{\rm p}}=\Omega\cap(-\pi,\pi]^{2}\times(-1,\infty).

We regard
\Omega_{{\rm p}}
as a submanifold of
\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}
, where
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}
is the flat torus. By definition,
\Omega_{\rm p}
is open and connected in
\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}
. Moreover,
\Omega_{{\rm p}}\cap\{x_{3}<2\}
is diffeomorphic to a bounded John domain in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We thus have the Bogovskii operator on
\Omega_{{\rm p}}\cap\{x_{3}<2\}
. It is important to notice that there is a one-to-one correspondence between the functions in
\Omega_{\rm p}
and the
(2\pi\mathbb{Z})^{2}
-periodic functions in
\Omega
. We say a function
F
defined in
\Omega
is
(2\pi\mathbb{Z})^{2}
-periodic if
F(x)=F(x+z)
for any
x\in\Omega
and
z\in(2\pi\mathbb{Z})^{2}\times\{0\}
. In other words, if
f\in L^{2}_{\rm loc}(\Omega_{\rm p})
, then there exists a locally
L^{2}
function
F
defined in
\Omega
so that

F(x)=f(\tilde{x})
, where
\tilde{x}
is the representation in
\Omega_{\rm p}
so that
x-\tilde{x}\in(2\pi\mathbb{Z})^{2}\times\{0\}
. In this sense and for convenience, we do not distinguish between
F
and
f
.

Denote by
L^{2}(\Omega_{\rm p})
and
\widehat{H}^{1}_{0}(\Omega_{\rm p})
the closure
of
C_{0}^{\infty}(\Omega_{\rm p})
under the norms

\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\rm p})}:=\bigg(\int_{\Omega_{\rm p}}|f|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2},\hskip 20.00003pt\|f\|_{\widehat{H}^{1}(\Omega_{\rm p})}:=\bigg(\int_{\Omega_{\rm p}}|\nabla f|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}.

Clearly,
\widehat{H}^{1}_{0}(\Omega_{\rm p})
is a Hilbert space with respect to the inner product
\langle\nabla f,\nabla g\rangle_{\Omega_{{\rm p}}}
.
Here and below,

\langle f,g\rangle_{\Omega_{{\rm p}}}:=\int_{\Omega_{{\rm p}}}f\cdot g.

Let
\widehat{H}^{1}_{0,\sigma}(\Omega_{{\rm p}})
be the subspace of
{\widehat{H}^{1}_{0}(\Omega_{\rm p})^{3}}
that consists of all the divergence-free functions, namely,
\widehat{H}^{1}_{0,\sigma}(\Omega_{{\rm p}})=\{f\in{\widehat{H}^{1}_{0}(\Omega_{{\rm p}})^{3}}~|~\nabla\cdot f=0\}
.

We now recall the Fourier series representation for the solutions of (
BL

⁢
1
s
t

(
j
)

) on the flat half-space
\{x_{3}>0\}
. The same formulas are obtained in
[
53
, Proposition 3]
based on the periodic Poisson kernel.
Note that
[
53
]
uses the fact that the equations are imposed on a domain whose boundary is given by the graph, but a similar proof is valid if we utilize the zero extension of the functions.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
L\in(0,\infty)
and
\Omega
be a periodic bumpy John domain with constant
L
according to Definition
1.3
. Then the weak solution
(v^{(1j)},q^{(1j)})
of (
BL

⁢
1
s
t

(
j
)

) given by Theorem
4.1
satisfies the following.

(i)

(v^{(1j)},q^{(1j)})
is expanded in Fourier series in
\{x_{3}>0\}
as

\begin{split}v^{(1j)}(x)&=\hat{v}^{(1j)}_{(0,0)}+\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}}\Big(\hat{v}^{(1j)}_{k}+\begin{pmatrix}-ik\\
|k|\end{pmatrix}V^{(1j)}(k)x_{3}\Big)e^{-|k|x_{3}}e^{ik\cdot x^{\prime}},\\
q^{(1j)}(x)&=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}}2|k|V^{(1j)}(k)e^{-|k|x_{3}}e^{ik\cdot x^{\prime}},\end{split}

(4.22)

where
V^{(1j)}(k)
is a scalar function of
k
defined by

\begin{split}V^{(1j)}(k)=\hat{v}^{(1j)}_{k,3}-i\frac{k}{|k|}\cdot(\hat{v}^{(1j)}_{k})^{\prime},\end{split}

(4.23)

and moreover,
\hat{v}^{(1j)}_{k}
is the Fourier series coefficient of
v^{(1j)}(x^{\prime},0)
:

\displaystyle\hat{v}^{(1j)}_{k}=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{(-\pi,\pi)^{2}}v^{(1j)}(x^{\prime},0)e^{-ik\cdot x^{\prime}}\,{\rm d}x^{\prime},\hskip 10.00002ptk\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}.

(4.24)

(ii)

The third component of
\hat{v}^{(1j)}_{(0,0)}
is zero. Particularly, by setting

\displaystyle\hat{v}^{(1j)}_{(0,0)}=:\alpha^{(1j)}=(\alpha^{(1j)}_{1},\alpha^{(1j)}_{2},0),

we have the exponential convergence

\begin{split}&|v^{(1j)}(x)-\alpha^{(1j)}|+|\nabla v^{(1j)}(x)|+|q(x)|\\
&\qquad\leq C\|v^{(1j)}(\cdot,0)\|_{L^{2}((-\pi,\pi)^{2})}\,e^{-x_{3}/2}\quad\text{for }x_{3}>1.\end{split}

(4.25)

Here
C
is a universal constant.

Construction of
v^{(2j)}
for
j\in\{1,3,5,6\}

We construct the second-order boundary layers
v^{(2j)}
corresponding to
P^{(2j)}=P^{(2j)}(x)
for
j\in\{1,3,5,6\}
. These boundary layers are solutions to (
BL

⁢
2
n
d

(
j
)

) with subquadratic growth; see Theorem
4.3
. We begin with the case
j=1
, where
P^{(21)}(x)=x_{2}x_{3}{\bf e}_{3}
. We recall that
(v^{(21)},q^{(21)})
solves

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\Delta v+\nabla q=0&\text{in }\Omega\\
\nabla\cdot v=0&\text{in }\Omega\\
v+x_{2}x_{3}{\bf e}_{1}=0&\text{on }\partial\Omega.\end{array}\right.

(BL
{}_{{\rm 2nd}}^{(1)}
)

The difficulty in the analysis of (
BL

⁢
2
n
d

(
1
)

) is that the boundary value is not periodic and has linear growth as
x_{2}\to\infty
. We aim at eliminating the growth factor
x_{2}
and at recovering the periodic structure. The key finding is the connection between the first-order and second-order boundary layers on the boundary, namely

v^{(21)}-x_{2}v^{(11)}=0,\hskip 10.00002ptx\in\partial\Omega.

(4.26)

This observation is the basis of the Ansatz for
v^{(21)}
.
Recall that
v^{(11)}
converges exponentially fast to the constant
\alpha^{(11)}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, when
x_{3}\rightarrow\infty
by the spectral gap near frequency
0
yielded by the periodicity; see (
4.25
).
Hence the non-decaying divergence
\nabla\cdot(x_{2}v^{(11)}(x))=v^{(11)}_{2}(x)
can be corrected by adding a corrector
-\alpha^{(11)}_{2}x_{3}\eta_{+}(x_{3}){\bf e}_{3}
.
Here
\eta_{+}(\cdot)
is a function on
\mathbb{R}
satisfying

\displaystyle\text{$\eta_{+}(t)$ is smooth and non-negative,}

(4.27)

\displaystyle\text{$\eta_{+}(t)=0$ if $t<\frac{1}{2}$ and $\eta_{+}(t)=1$ if $t>1$}.

Below, we also need the cut-off
\eta_{-}
defined in (
4.8
).

The following statement gives the existence and the structure of second-order boundary layers with subquadratic growth.

Theorem 4.3
.

Let
L\in(0,\infty)
and
\Omega
be a periodic bumpy John domain with constant
L
according to Definition
1.3
. There exists a weak solution
(v^{(21)},q^{(21)})\in H^{1}_{{\rm loc}}(\overline{\Omega})^{3}\times L^{2}_{{\rm loc}}(\overline{\Omega})
to (
BL

⁢
2
n
d

(
1
)

) decomposed as

\displaystyle v^{(21)}(x)

\displaystyle=x_{2}v^{(11)}(x)-\alpha^{(11)}_{2}x_{3}\eta_{+}(x_{3}){\bf e}_{3}+R^{(21)}(x),

(4.28)

\displaystyle q^{(21)}(x)

\displaystyle=x_{2}q^{(11)}(x)+Q^{(21)}(x),

where
(R^{(21)},Q^{(21)})\in\widehat{H}_{0}^{1}(\Omega_{\rm p})^{3}\times L^{2}(\Omega_{\rm p})
. Moreover, we have

\|\nabla R^{(21)}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{{\rm p}})}+\|Q^{(21)}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{{\rm p}})}\leq C,

(4.29)

where the constant
C
depends only on
L
.

Proof.

We aim at proving the existence of
(R^{(21)},Q^{(21)})
and estimating it so that
(v^{(21)},q^{(21)})
defined by the right-hand sides in (
4.28
) gives a weak solution of (
BL

⁢
2
n
d

(
1
)

).

(
Existence
)
By the previous discussion, we begin with a formal examination of
x_{2}v^{(11)}(x)-\alpha^{(11)}_{2}x_{3}\eta_{+}(x_{3}){\bf e}_{3}
. First of all, it is easy to see

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\big(x_{2}v^{(11)}(x)-\alpha^{(11)}_{2}x_{3}\eta_{+}(x_{3}){\bf e}_{3}\big)

\displaystyle=v^{(11)}_{2}(x)-\alpha^{(11)}_{2}\eta_{+}(x_{3})-\alpha^{(11)}_{2}x_{3}\partial_{3}\eta_{+}(x_{3})

for all
x\in\Omega_{\rm p}
. Notice that the expression above simplifies for
x_{3}>1
:

\nabla\cdot\big(x_{2}v^{(11)}(x)-\alpha^{(11)}_{2}x_{3}\eta_{+}(x_{3}){\bf e}_{3}\big)=v^{(11)}_{2}(x)-\alpha^{(11)}_{2}.

Then, by Proposition
4.2
, we get

\displaystyle v^{(11)}_{2}(x)-\alpha^{(11)}_{2}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}}\big(\hat{v}^{(11)}_{k,2}-ik_{2}V^{(11)}(k)x_{3}\big)e^{-|k|x_{3}}e^{ik\cdot x^{\prime}},\hskip 10.00002ptx_{3}>1.

This means that
x_{2}v^{(11)}(x)-\alpha^{(11)}_{2}x_{3}\eta_{+}(x_{3}){\bf e}_{3}
is not divergence-free. Thus, our next goal is to construct a function to correct the divergence for
x_{3}>1
.
Define

\begin{split}d(x)&:=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2},\,k_{1}\neq 0}\Big(\frac{1}{ik_{1}}\big(\hat{v}^{(11)}_{k,2}-ik_{2}V^{(11)}(k)x_{3}\big)e^{-|k|x_{3}}e^{ik\cdot x^{\prime}}\Big){\bf e}_{1}\\
&\quad+\sum_{k_{1}=0,\,k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\Big(\frac{1}{ik_{2}}\big(\hat{v}^{(11)}_{k,2}-ik_{2}V^{(11)}(k)x_{3}\big)e^{-|k|x_{3}}e^{ik\cdot x^{\prime}}\Big){\bf e}_{2},\end{split}

(4.30)

which is an element of

H^{1}(\Omega_{{\rm p},>0})^{3}
where
\Omega_{{\rm p},>0}:=\Omega_{{\rm p}}\cap\{x_{3}>0\}
. Of course, there is no unique way to construct a right-inverse of the divergence such as
d
.
We may extend
d(x)
to the whole domain
\Omega_{\rm p}
by multiplying it by
\eta_{+}(x_{3})
and still correct the divergence of
x_{2}v^{(11)}(x)-\alpha^{(11)}_{2}x_{3}\eta_{+}(x_{3}){\bf e}_{3}-d(x)\eta_{+}(x_{3})
.
To check the divergence condition, we calculate

\displaystyle D(x)

\displaystyle:=\nabla\cdot\big(x_{2}v^{(11)}(x)-\alpha^{(11)}_{2}x_{3}\eta_{+}(x_{3}){\bf e}_{3}-d(x)\eta_{+}(x_{3})\big)

\displaystyle=v^{(11)}_{2}(x)-\alpha^{(11)}_{2}\eta_{+}(x_{3})-\eta_{+}(x_{3})\nabla\cdot d(x)-(\alpha^{(11)}_{2}x_{3}+d_{3}(x))\partial_{3}\eta_{+}(x_{3}).

Obviously,
D
is supported in
\Omega_{{\rm p},<2}:=\Omega_{{\rm p}}\cap\{x_{3}<2\}
, in which we can rely on the Bogovskii operator to find a right-inverse of the divergence.
Let
A:=\int_{\Omega_{{\rm p},<2}}D
. Let
\chi_{+}(x_{3})
be a smooth cut-off function such that

\chi_{+}(x_{3})=0\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{if}\hskip 10.00002ptx_{3}\leq 0,\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\chi_{+}(x_{3})=(2\pi)^{-2}A\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{for}\hskip 10.00002ptx_{3}>1.

(4.31)

This implies
\partial_{3}\chi_{+}(x_{3})
is supported in
\Omega_{{\rm p},<2}
and
\int_{\Omega_{{\rm p},<2}}\partial_{3}\chi_{+}=A
. It follows that

\int_{\Omega_{{\rm p},<2}}\big(D(x)-\nabla\cdot\big(\chi_{+}(x_{3}){\bf e}_{3}\big)\big)\,dx=\int_{\Omega_{{\rm p},<2}}\big(D(x)-\partial_{3}\chi_{+}(x_{3})\big)\,dx=0.

Hence, by Appendix
A
, there is a Bogovskii corrector
\mathbb{B}\in H_{0}^{1}(\Omega_{{\rm p},<2})^{3}
such that

\nabla\cdot\mathbb{B}(x)=D(x)-\partial_{3}\chi_{+}(x_{3}),

and
\|\mathbb{B}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega_{{\rm p},<2})}\leq C
, where
C
depends only on the John constant
L
of
\Omega
.
We extend
\mathbb{B}
by zero to the whole domain
\Omega_{\rm p}
and denote it again by

\mathbb{B}\in{H^{1}_{0}(\Omega_{{\rm p}})^{3}}
.
Let us combine the above correctors and define

\mathcal{C}(x)=-d(x)\eta_{+}(x_{3})-\chi_{+}(x_{3}){\bf e}_{3}-\mathbb{B}(x).

Note that
\mathcal{C}\in{\widehat{H}_{0}^{1}(\Omega_{\rm p})^{3}}
. In particular,
\|\nabla\mathcal{C}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{{\rm p}})}\leq C
, where
C
depends only on the John constant
L
of
\Omega
. By (
4.25
), the function
\mathcal{C}
converges exponentially fast to
-(2\pi)^{-2}A
as
x_{3}\to\infty
, and its derivatives decay exponentially fast to
0
as
x_{3}\rightarrow\infty
.

By the crucial cancellation

x_{2}(-\Delta v^{(11)}(x)+\nabla q^{(11)}(x))=0,

as well as the definition of
\mathcal{C}
, we see that the pair

x_{2}v^{(11)}(x)-\alpha^{(11)}_{2}x_{3}\eta_{+}(x_{3}){\bf e}_{3}+\mathcal{C}(x)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptx_{2}q^{(11)}(x)

is a weak solution to (
BL

⁢
2
n
d

(
1
)

) with an additional external force

f^{(21)}(x)=-2\partial_{2}v^{(11)}(x)+q^{(11)}(x){\bf e}_{2}-\Delta\big(-\alpha^{(11)}_{2}x_{3}\eta_{+}(x_{3}){\bf e}_{3}+\mathcal{C}(x)\big).

In order to cancel this source term, we consider

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\Delta\mathcal{R}^{(21)}+\nabla\mathcal{Q}^{(21)}=-f^{(21)}&\text{in }\Omega_{\rm p}\\
\nabla\cdot\mathcal{R}^{(21)}=0&\text{in }\Omega_{\rm p}\\
\mathcal{R}^{(21)}=0&\text{on }\partial\Omega_{\rm p}.\end{array}\right.

(4.32)

The weak formulation of (
4.32
) is written as

\langle\nabla\mathcal{R}^{(21)},\nabla\varphi\rangle_{\Omega_{{\rm p}}}=-\langle f^{(21)},\varphi\rangle_{\Omega_{{\rm p}}},\hskip 10.00002pt\varphi\in\widehat{H}^{1}_{0,\sigma}(\Omega_{{\rm p}}).

(4.33)

Next, we prove the unique existence of the weak solution of (
4.33
). By the integration by parts for
\Delta\mathcal{C}
,

\displaystyle\langle f^{(21)},\varphi\rangle_{\Omega_{{\rm p}}}

\displaystyle=-2\langle\partial_{2}v^{(11)},\varphi\rangle_{\Omega_{{\rm p}}}+\langle q^{(11)},\varphi_{2}\rangle_{\Omega_{{\rm p}}}

(4.34)

\displaystyle+\alpha^{(11)}_{2}\langle\Delta(x_{3}\eta_{+}(x_{3}){\bf e}_{3}),\varphi\big\rangle_{\Omega_{{\rm p}}}+\langle\nabla\mathcal{C},\nabla\varphi\rangle_{\Omega_{{\rm p}}}.

By the Poincaré inequality in
\Omega_{{\rm p},<2}
and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality in
\Omega_{\rm p}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&|\langle f^{(21)},\varphi\rangle_{\Omega_{{\rm p}}}|\\
&\leq C\Big(\|\nabla v^{(11)}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{{\rm p}})}+\|q^{(11)}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{{\rm p}})}+\|\Delta(x_{3}\eta_{+}(x_{3})\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\rm p})}+\|\nabla\mathcal{C}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\rm p})}\Big)\|\nabla\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{{\rm p}})}\\
&\qquad+\bigg|\int_{1}^{\infty}\int_{(-\pi,\pi)^{2}}\partial_{2}v^{(11)}(x)\varphi(x)\,\,{\rm d}x^{\prime}\,{\rm d}x_{3}\bigg|\\
&\qquad+\bigg|\int_{1}^{\infty}\int_{(-\pi,\pi)^{2}}q^{(11)}(x)\varphi_{2}(x)\,\,{\rm d}x^{\prime}\,{\rm d}x_{3}\bigg|.\end{split}

(4.35)

From Proposition
4.2
again, we have the following representation formulas:

\displaystyle\partial_{2}v^{(11)}(y)

\displaystyle=\partial_{2}\bigg(\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}}\Big(\hat{v}^{(1j)}_{k}+\begin{pmatrix}-ik\\
|k|\end{pmatrix}V^{(1j)}(k)x_{3}\Big)e^{-|k|x_{3}}e^{ik\cdot x^{\prime}}\bigg),

\displaystyle q^{(11)}(y)

\displaystyle=\partial_{1}\bigg(\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2},\,k_{1}\neq 0}2|k|V^{(1j)}(k)e^{-|k|x_{3}}\frac{e^{ik\cdot x^{\prime}}}{ik_{1}}\bigg)

\displaystyle+\partial_{2}\bigg(\sum_{k_{1}=0,\,k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}2|k|V^{(1j)}(k)e^{-|k|x_{3}}\frac{e^{ik\cdot x^{\prime}}}{ik_{2}}\bigg).

Thus, by integration by parts in
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
, the last two integrals in (
4.35
) are bounded by
C\|\nabla\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{{\rm p}})}
. Consequently, in view of (
4.6
) and (
4.35
), we obtain

\displaystyle|\langle f^{(21)},\varphi\rangle_{\Omega_{{\rm p}}}|\leq C\|\nabla\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{{\rm p}})}.

(4.36)

Then, by the Riesz representation theorem, there is an element
\mathcal{R}^{(21)}\in\widehat{H}^{1}_{0,\sigma}(\Omega_{{\rm p}})
solving (
4.33
) and satisfying
\|\nabla\mathcal{R}^{(21)}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{{\rm p}})}\leq C
. The existence of the pressure
\mathcal{Q}^{(21)}\in L^{2}_{{\rm loc}}(\overline{\Omega}_{\rm p})
can be proved by using the Bogovskii lemma. Finally, the existence of the remainder
(R^{(21)},Q^{(21)})
in (
4.28
) is proved if we set
R^{(21)}=\mathcal{C}^{(21)}+\mathcal{R}^{(21)}
and
Q^{(21)}=\mathcal{Q}^{(21)}
. Let us emphasize that
(R^{(21)},Q^{(21)})
can be “unwrapped” (without changing the notation) as a
(2\pi\mathbb{Z})^{2}
-periodic function in
\Omega
.

(
Estimate
) The estimate of
R^{(21)}
is clear. Hence we focus on the pressure term
Q^{(21)}=\mathcal{Q}^{(21)}
. Notice that since
\mathcal{Q}^{(21)}
is locally
L^{2}
, it suffices to prove

\|\mathcal{Q}^{(21)}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{{\rm p},>3})}\leq C.

(4.37)

We apply the Fourier series expansion in the flat domain
\{x_{3}>3\}
. We decompose
\mathcal{R}^{(21)}
and
\mathcal{Q}^{(21)}
into
\mathcal{R}^{(21)}=w_{1}+w_{2}
and
\mathcal{Q}^{(21)}=r_{1}+r_{2}
(up to a constant), where
(w_{1},r_{1})
is a solution of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\Delta w_{1}+\nabla r_{1}=0,&x_{3}>3\\
\nabla\cdot w_{1}=0,&x_{3}>3\\
w_{1}(x^{\prime},3)=\mathcal{R}^{(21)}(x^{\prime},3),\end{array}\right.

while
(w_{2},r_{2})
solves

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\Delta w_{2}+\nabla r_{2}=-f^{(21)},&x_{3}>3\\
\nabla\cdot w_{2}=0,&x_{3}>3\\
w_{2}(x^{\prime},3)=0.\end{array}\right.

Using the periodicity of
\mathcal{R}^{(21)}(x^{\prime},3)
in
x^{\prime}
, the solution
(w_{1},r_{1})
may be written by the Poisson kernel as in Proposition
4.2
, which implies

\|r_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{{\rm p},>3})}\leq C\|\nabla\mathcal{R}^{(21)}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{{\rm p}})}\leq C.

(4.38)

On the other hand, observe that the source term
-f^{(21)}
is represented as

-f^{(21)}(x)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}}(\mathcal{F}_{1}(k)+\mathcal{F}_{2}(k)x_{3})e^{-|k|x_{3}}e^{ik\cdot x^{\prime}},\hskip 10.00002ptx_{3}>3,

where

|\mathcal{F}_{1}(k)|+|\mathcal{F}_{2}(k)|\leq C|k|^{2}|\hat{v}^{(11)}_{k}|

with
\hat{v}^{(1j)}_{k}
defined in (
4.24
). Then a simple computation shows that

\displaystyle w_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}}(\mathcal{G}_{1}(k)+\mathcal{G}_{2}(k)x_{3}+\mathcal{G}_{3}(k)x_{3}^{2}+\mathcal{G}_{4}(k)x_{3}^{3})e^{-|k|x_{3}}e^{ik\cdot x^{\prime}},

\displaystyle r_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}}(\mathcal{G}_{5}(k)+\mathcal{G}_{6}(k)x_{3}+\mathcal{G}_{7}(k)x_{3}^{2}+\mathcal{G}_{8}(k)x_{3}^{3})e^{-|k|x_{3}}e^{ik\cdot x^{\prime}},

where

|k|\sum_{l=1}^{4}|\mathcal{G}_{l}(k)|+\sum_{l=5}^{8}|\mathcal{G}_{l}(k)|\leq C|k|^{3}|\hat{v}^{(11)}_{k}|.

Now it is easy to see that

\|r_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{{\rm p},>3})}\leq C\|v^{(11)}(\cdot,0)\|_{L^{2}((-\pi,\pi)^{2})}\leq C.

(4.39)

From (
4.38
) and (
4.39
), we obtain (
4.37
). This completes the proof of Theorem
4.3
.
∎

By a similar consideration, we can obtain the existence of
(v^{(2j)},q^{(2j)})
for
j\in\{3,5,6\}
, whose proofs are parallel to Theorem
4.3
and therefore omitted.
Recall that
\eta_{+}
(resp.
\eta_{-}
) is defined in (
4.27
) (resp. (
4.8
)).

.

Let
L\in(0,\infty)
and
\Omega
be a periodic bumpy John domain with constant

according to Definition
1.3
. Let
j\in\{3,5,6\}
. There exists a weak solution
(v^{(2j)},q^{(2j)})\in H^{1}_{{\rm loc}}(\overline{\Omega})^{3}\times L^{2}_{{\rm loc}}(\overline{\Omega})
to (
BL

⁢
2
n
d

(
j
)

) decomposed as, when
j=3
,

\displaystyle v^{(23)}(x)

\displaystyle=x_{1}v^{(12)}(x)-\alpha^{(12)}_{1}x_{3}\eta_{+}(x_{3}){\bf e}_{3}+R^{(23)}(x),

(4.40)

\displaystyle q^{(23)}(x)

\displaystyle=x_{1}q^{(12)}(x)+Q^{(23)}(x),

when
j=5
,

\displaystyle v^{(25)}(x)

\displaystyle=-2x_{1}v^{(11)}(x)-x_{3}^{2}\eta_{-}(x_{3}){\bf e}_{3}+2\alpha^{(11)}_{1}x_{3}\eta_{+}(x_{3}){\bf e}_{3}+R^{(25)}(x),

. Moreover, we have

(4.43)

.

Construction of
v^{(22)}
and
v^{(24)}

The boundary layers corresponding to
P^{(22)}
and
P^{(24)}
can be constructed by using the Green function. The fact that

and

only depend on the vertical variable
x_{3}
and that there is no growth in the tangential variable
x^{\prime}
makes the analysis much easier than for
P^{(2j)}
,
j\in\{1,3,5,6\}
studied above.
The proof of the following proposition is almost identical to the one of Theorem
4.1
. Notice that here we state Theorem
4.5
in the periodic case only for convenience. Indeed we use these correctors in combination with
(v^{(2j)},q^{(2j)})
for
j\in{\{1,3,5,6\}}
whose existence is stated in

4.3
and
4.4
in periodic bumpy John domains. However, the existence of
(v^{(2j)},q^{(2j)})
for
j\in\{2,4\}
can be proved in general bumpy John domains according to Definition
1.2
.

.

s

)

,

5.
Higher-order regularity

5.1.
Large-scale
C^{1,\gamma}
estimate

The goal of this subsection is to prove the large-scale
C^{1,\gamma}
regularity stated in Theorem
B
. We will use the first-order boundary layers and modify the argument of the Lipschitz estimate.

Recall that
\mathscr{P}_{1}={\rm span}\{P^{(11)},P^{(12)}\}=\{(ax_{3},bx_{3},0)~|~a,b\in\mathbb{R}\}
. Let
\mathscr{P}_{2}=\text{span}\{P^{(2j)}~|~j=1,2,\cdots,6\}
. Let
\mathscr{S}_{2}=\text{span}\{(P^{(2j)},L^{(2j)})~|~j=1,2,\cdots,6\}
. Note that any element of
\mathscr{S}_{2}
is a weak solution of the Stokes system in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Let
(v^{(1k)},q^{(1k)})
with
k=1,2
, and
(v^{(2j)},q^{(2j)})
with
j=1,2,\cdots,6
, be the first-order and second-order boundary layers, respectively. Then define

\displaystyle\mathscr{Q}_{1}(\Omega)

\displaystyle=\text{span}\{(P^{(1k)},0)+(v^{(1k)},q^{(1k)})~|~k=1,2\},

(5.1)

\displaystyle\mathscr{Q}_{2}(\Omega)

\displaystyle=\text{span}\{(P^{(2j)},{L^{(2j)}})+(v^{(2j)},q^{(2j)})~|~j=1,2,\cdots,6\}.

(5.2)

Hence,
\mathscr{Q}_{1}(\Omega)
(resp.
\mathscr{Q}_{2}(\Omega)
) is the vector space that contains all the “linear” (resp. “quadratic”) solutions of the Stokes system in
\Omega
vanishing on the boundary
\partial\Omega
; see the Liouville-type results at the end of this section stated in Theorem
5.8
.

Remark 5.1
.

Note that the pressure part in estimate (
1.3
) of Theorem
B
is different from the Lipschitz estimate in which
\bar{P}
is
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{1/2,+}^{\varepsilon}}p^{\varepsilon}
. Actually, in (
1.3
),
\bar{P}_{1}
is the average of the corrected pressure over a small ball, i.e.,

\bar{P}_{1}=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{O(\varepsilon),+}^{\varepsilon}}\big(p^{\varepsilon}-\pi(x/\varepsilon)\big)\,{\rm d}x

for some
(w,\pi)\in\mathscr{Q}_{1}(\Omega)
; see (
5.15
). This is reasonable since we are concerned with the
C^{0,\gamma}
estimate of the pressure and
\bar{P}_{1}
plays a role similar to
the zeroth-order term in the Taylor expansion of the pressure, if the boundary is flat. We emphasize that
\bar{P}_{1}
depends on
\varepsilon
. The point here is that
\bar{P}_{1}
is independent of
r
.

The critical fact we are going to use is that any
(w,\pi)\in\mathscr{Q}_{1}(\Omega)

is a solution of the Stokes system in
\Omega
that vanishes on
\partial\Omega
. Hence, by rescaling,
(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon})-(\varepsilon w(x/\varepsilon),\pi(x/\varepsilon))
is still a weak solution with a no-slip boundary condition. This observation allows us to capture the regularity beyond the Lipschitz estimate. To this end, we define the first-order excess by

\displaystyle H_{{\rm 1st}}(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};\rho)

(5.3)

\displaystyle=\inf_{(w,\pi)\in\mathscr{Q}_{1}(\Omega)}\bigg\{\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{\rho,+}}\big|\nabla u^{\varepsilon}-\nabla\big(\varepsilon w(x/\varepsilon)\big)\big|^{2}\,{\rm d}x\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle+\sup_{s,t\in[1/16,1/4]}\bigg|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{s\rho,+}}\big(p^{\varepsilon}-\pi(x/\varepsilon)\big)\,{\rm d}x-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{t\rho,+}}\big(p^{\varepsilon}-\pi(x/\varepsilon)\big)\,{\rm d}x\bigg|\bigg\}.

Recall that
(w,\pi)\in\mathscr{Q}_{1}(\Omega)
means that

(w,\pi)=\sum_{k=1}^{2}\ell_{k}(P^{(1k)}+v^{(1k)},q^{(1k)}),

for some
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
.
We will also use the quantity
\Phi(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};\rho)
defined in (
3.8
).

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
L\in(0,\infty)
and
\Omega
be a bumpy John domain with constant
L
according to Definition
1.2
.
Let
(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon})
be as in Theorem
B
, namely, a weak solution of (
Sε
) in Subsection
3.1
satisfying (
1.1
).
For all
\varepsilon\in(0,{\frac{1}{32}}]
,
r\in{[2\varepsilon,\frac{1}{16}}]
and
\theta\in(0,{\frac{1}{8}}]
,

\displaystyle H_{{\rm 1st}}(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};\theta r)

\displaystyle\leq C\big(\theta+\theta^{-3}\big(\frac{\varepsilon}{r}\big)^{1/12}\big)\Phi(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};16r)

(5.4)

\displaystyle+C\theta^{-3}\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{{Q_{10r}}}|\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\varepsilon}[F^{\varepsilon}]|^{3}\bigg)^{1/3},

where
C
depends only on
L
.

Proof.

First, we apply Lemma
3.7
with
\alpha=1

\displaystyle H(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};\theta r)

\displaystyle\leq C\big(\theta+\theta^{-3}\big(\frac{\varepsilon}{r}\big)^{1/12}\big)\Phi(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};16r)

(5.5)

\displaystyle+C\theta^{-3}\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{{Q_{10r}}}|\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\varepsilon}[F^{\varepsilon}]|^{3}\bigg)^{1/3},

where we also used the fact

H(\cdot,\cdot,2r)\leq\Phi(\cdot,\cdot,2r)\leq C\Phi(\cdot,\cdot,16r)
.
Let
P^{*}=\ell_{1}^{*}P^{(11)}+\ell_{2}^{*}P^{(12)}\in\mathscr{P}_{1}
be the linear solution that minimizes
H(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};\theta r)
. Then (
3.31
) implies

\sum_{k=1}^{2}|\ell_{k}^{*}|\leq C\big(H(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};\theta r)+\Phi(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};\theta r)\big)\leq C{\theta^{-3/2}}\Phi(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};r).

(5.6)

By the definition of
H_{\rm 1st}
and
H
, one has

\displaystyle H_{\rm 1st}(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon},\theta r)

(5.7)

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{\theta r,+}}\Big|\nabla u^{\varepsilon}-\nabla\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{2}{\ell}^{*}_{k}(P^{(1k)}+\varepsilon v^{(1k)}(x/\varepsilon)\Big)\Big|^{2}\,{\rm d}x\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle+\sup_{s,t\in[1/16,1/4]}\bigg|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{s\theta r,+}}\Big(p^{\varepsilon}-\sum_{k=1}^{2}{\ell}^{*}_{k}q^{(1k)}(x/\varepsilon)\Big)\,{\rm d}x-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{t\theta r,+}}\Big(p^{\varepsilon}-\sum_{k=1}^{2}{\ell}^{*}_{k}q^{(1k)}(x/\varepsilon)\Big)\,{\rm d}x\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq H(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};\theta r)+\sum_{k=1}^{2}|\ell^{*}_{k}|\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{\theta r,+}}|(\nabla v^{(1k)})(x/\varepsilon)|^{2}\,{\rm d}x\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle+2\sup_{\rho\in[1/16,1/4]}\sum_{k=1}^{2}|\ell^{*}_{k}|\ \bigg|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{\rho\theta r,+}^{\varepsilon}}q^{(1k)}(x/\varepsilon)\,{\rm d}x-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{\theta r/2,+}^{\varepsilon}}q^{(1k)}(x/\varepsilon)\,{\rm d}x\bigg|.

From Proposition
4.6
, we have the estimate for the first-order boundary layers

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{2}\bigg\{\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{\theta r,+}}|(\nabla v)^{(1k)}(x/\varepsilon)|^{2}\,{\rm d}x\bigg)^{1/2}+\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{\theta r,+}}|q^{(1k)}(x/\varepsilon)|^{2}\,{\rm d}x\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\}\leq C\theta^{-1/2}\big(\frac{\varepsilon}{r}\big)^{1/2}.

(5.8)

Inserting this into (
5.7
) and using (
5.6
) and (
5.5
), we obtain

\displaystyle H_{\rm 1st}(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};\theta r)

\displaystyle\leq H(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};\theta r)+C{\theta^{-3}}\big(\frac{\varepsilon}{r}\big)^{1/2}\Phi(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};r)

\displaystyle\leq C\big(\theta+\theta^{-3}\big(\frac{\varepsilon}{r}\big)^{1/12}\big)\Phi(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};16r)+C\theta^{-3}\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{{Q_{10r}}}|\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\varepsilon}[F^{\varepsilon}]|^{3}\bigg)^{1/3}.

This proves the lemma.
∎

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
L\in(0,\infty)
and
\Omega
be a bumpy John domain with constant
L
according to Definition
1.2
. Let
(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon})
be as in Theorem
B
. For any
\gamma\in[0,1)
,
\delta\in(0,1)
,
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]
and
r\in[\varepsilon,\frac{1}{2}]
,

\displaystyle H_{{\rm 1st}}(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};r)\leq Cr^{\gamma}(M+M^{4+2\gamma+\delta}),

(5.9)

where
C
depends on
L
,
\gamma
and
\delta
. Here
M
is the number given in Theorem
B
.

Proof.

Note that it suffices to prove (
5.9
) for
r\in[N_{0}\varepsilon,1/N_{1}]
with some absolute constant
N_{0},N_{1}\geq 2
. The cases for
r\in(1/N_{1},\frac{1}{2}]
or
\varepsilon\geq 1/(N_{0}N_{1})
follow directly from the Bogovskii lemma and the Poincaré inequality. The case
r\in[\varepsilon,N_{0}\varepsilon]
follows from the case
r=N_{0}\varepsilon
.

Firstly, using (
3.39
) with
\beta=\gamma+\delta
(with
\delta\in(0,{\frac{2-\gamma}{2}})
being arbitrary), we have

\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{{Q_{r}}}|\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\varepsilon}[F^{\varepsilon}]|^{3}\bigg)^{1/3}\leq C_{\delta}(M+M^{4+2\gamma+4\delta})r^{\gamma+\delta}

(5.10)

with
C_{\delta}
depending on
\delta
.

Since
\theta\in(0,{\frac{1}{8}}]
in Lemma
5.2
is arbitrary, we can choose
\theta
sufficiently small so that
C\theta\leq\frac{1}{2}(\frac{\theta}{16})^{\gamma}
holds in (
5.4
). For such fixed
\theta
, we can find
{\varepsilon_{0}}\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
depending on
\gamma
and
\theta
such that the factor in (
5.4
) satisfies

C\theta^{-3}\big(\frac{\varepsilon}{r}\big)^{{1/12}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\big(\frac{\theta}{16}\big)^{\gamma},\hskip 10.00002ptr\in[\varepsilon/{\varepsilon_{0}},\frac{1}{16}]

in (
5.4
). Then by (
5.10
) and (
5.4
),

\displaystyle H_{{\rm 1st}}(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};\theta r)

\displaystyle\leq\big(\frac{\theta}{16}\big)^{\gamma}\Phi(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};16r)+C_{\delta}(M+M^{4+2\gamma+4\delta})r^{\gamma+\delta}.

(5.11)

Now the key observation is that, for any
(w,\pi)\in\mathscr{Q}_{1}(\Omega)
, the pair
(U^{\varepsilon},\Pi^{\varepsilon})
defined by

U^{\varepsilon}(x)=u^{\varepsilon}(x)-\varepsilon w(x/\varepsilon),\hskip 20.00003pt\Pi^{\varepsilon}(x)=p^{\varepsilon}(x)-\pi(x/\varepsilon)

is still a weak solution of
the Stokes system (
Sε
) in Subsection
3.1
.
Therefore, the estimate (
5.11
) still holds if we replace
\Phi(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};16r)
by
\Phi(U^{\varepsilon},\Pi^{\varepsilon};16r)
for any
(w,\pi)\in\mathscr{Q}_{1}(\Omega)
.
Then taking the infimum over all
(w,\pi)\in\mathscr{Q}_{1}(\Omega)
, we can further replace
\Phi(U^{\varepsilon},\Pi^{\varepsilon};16r)
by
H_{{\rm 1st}}(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};16r)
. Hence we obtain

\displaystyle H_{{\rm 1st}}(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};\theta r)\leq\big(\frac{\theta}{16})^{\gamma}H_{{\rm 1st}}(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};16r)+C_{\delta}(M+M^{4+2\gamma+4\delta})r^{\gamma+\delta}.

(5.12)

This is the first-order excess decay estimate for the
C^{1,\gamma}
regularity of
(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon})
. Note that we can eventually replace
4\delta
by
\delta
in the right hand side of (
5.12
) as
\delta\in(0,1)
is arbitrary. Thus, by a simple iteration, we have that for
\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{0}\leq r\leq\frac{\theta}{16}
,

H_{{\rm 1st}}(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};r)\leq r^{\gamma}\big(H_{{\rm 1st}}(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};r_{0})+C_{\delta}(M+M^{4+2\gamma+\delta})\big),

for some
r_{0}\in[\frac{\theta}{16},1]
. Clearly,
H_{{\rm 1st}}(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};r_{0})\leq\Phi(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};r_{0})
. It remains to show

\Phi(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};r_{0})\leq C(M+M^{2}).

Indeed, since
r_{0}
is comparable to
1
, the above estimate follows directly from the Poincaré inequality and Bogovskii’s lemma. The proof is complete.
∎

The above theorem directly implies the
C^{1,\gamma}
estimate for the velocity. To handle the pressure estimate in Theorem
B
, we need the following lemma.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
L\in(0,\infty)
and
\Omega
be a bumpy John domain with constant
L
according to Definition
1.2
. For a given
\rho>0
, let
(\ell_{1}(\rho),\ell_{2}(\rho))
be the pair of real numbers so that

(w,\pi)=\sum_{k=1}^{2}\ell_{k}(\rho)(P^{(1k)}+v^{(1k)},q^{(1k)})

minimizes
H_{{\rm 1st}}(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};\rho)
. Then
there exists a constant
\varepsilon_{1}\in(0,1)
so that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{1}]

and
r\in[\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{1},{\frac{1}{2}}]
,

\sup_{r_{1},r_{2}\in[r,2r]}\sum_{k=1}^{2}|\ell_{k}(r_{1})-\ell_{k}(r_{2})|\leq C\sup_{t\in[r,2r]}H_{{\rm 1st}}(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};t),

(5.13)

where
C
depends only on
L
.

Proof.

By the definition of
H_{\rm 1st}
, the triangle inequality and using that
the matrices
\nabla P^{(1k)}

are linearly independent over
\mathbb{R}
, if
r\leq r_{1},r_{2}\leq 2r
,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{2}|\ell_{k}(r_{1})-\ell_{k}(r_{2})|

\displaystyle\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{r,+}}\Big|\sum_{k=1}^{2}(\ell_{k}(r_{1})-\ell_{k}(r_{2}))\nabla P^{(1k)}\Big|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{r,+}}\Big|\nabla\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{2}(\ell_{k}(r_{1})-\ell_{k}(r_{2}))(P^{(1k)}+\varepsilon v^{(1k)}(x/\varepsilon)\Big)\Big|^{2}\,{\rm d}x\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+C\sum_{k=1}^{2}|\ell_{k}(r_{1})-\ell_{k}(r_{2})|\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{r,+}}|(\nabla v^{(1k)})(x/\varepsilon)|^{2}\,{\rm d}x\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq CH_{{\rm 1st}}(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};r_{1})+CH_{{\rm 1st}}(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};r_{2})+C_{0}\big(\frac{\varepsilon}{r}\big)^{1/2}\sum_{k=1}^{2}|\ell_{k}(r_{1})-\ell_{k}(r_{2})|,

where in the last inequality, we inserted
u^{\varepsilon}
and enlarged the domain from
B_{r,+}^{\varepsilon}
to
B_{r_{i},+}^{\varepsilon}
with
i=1,2
, and applied Proposition
4.6
.
Now if
r\geq\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{1}
for some small
\varepsilon_{1}\in(0,1)
so that
C_{0}\varepsilon_{1}^{1/2}<\frac{1}{2}
, then

\sum_{k=1}^{2}|\ell_{k}(r_{1})-\ell_{k}(r_{2})|\leq C\sum_{i=1}^{2}H_{{\rm 1st}}(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};r_{i}).

This gives the desired estimate.
∎

Proof of Theorem
B
:

Let
\varepsilon_{1}\in(0,1)
be the number in Lemma
5.4
. Note that it suffices to prove (
1.3
) when

\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{1}]
and
r\in[\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{1},\frac{1}{16}]
as a familiar argument enables us to remove the smallness condition on
\varepsilon
and the restriction on
r
.
The velocity estimate in (
1.3
) follows from
the Poincaré inequality and
(
5.9
). Hence, it suffices to estimate the pressure. Let
(\ell_{1}(\rho),\ell_{2}(\rho))
be as in Lemma
5.4
. For
r\in[\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{1},{\frac{1}{16}}]
, let
K
be the integer so that
4^{-K}r\in[\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{1},4\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{1})
. By the triangle inequality, the estimate of
q^{(1j)}

in Proposition
4.6
,

\displaystyle\bigg|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{4^{-K}r,+}}\bigg(p^{\varepsilon}-\sum_{k=1}^{2}\ell_{k}(4^{1-K}r)q^{(1k)}(x/\varepsilon)\bigg)\,{\rm d}x-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{r,+}}\bigg(p^{\varepsilon}-\sum_{k=1}^{2}\ell_{k}(4r)q^{(1k)}(x/\varepsilon)\bigg)\,{\rm d}x\bigg|

(5.14)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{K}\bigg|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{4^{i-K-1}r,+}}\bigg(p^{\varepsilon}-\sum_{k=1}^{2}\ell_{k}(4^{i-K}r)q^{(1k)}(x/\varepsilon)\bigg)\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{4^{i-K}r,+}}\bigg(p^{\varepsilon}-\sum_{k=1}^{2}\ell_{k}(4^{i-K+1}r)q^{(1k)}(x/\varepsilon)\bigg)\,{\rm d}x\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{K}\Big(H_{\rm 1st}(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};4^{i-K+1}r)+\sum_{k=1}^{2}|\ell_{k}(4^{i-K+1}r)-\ell_{k}(4^{i-K}r)|\big(\frac{\varepsilon}{4^{i-K}r}\big)^{1/2}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{i=1}^{K}(4^{i-K+1}r)^{{\gamma}}(M+M^{4+2\gamma+\delta})

\displaystyle\leq Cr^{\gamma}(M+M^{4+2\gamma+\delta}),

where we have used (
5.9
) and (
5.13
) in the third inequality. Define

\bar{P}_{1}=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{1},+}}\bigg(p^{\varepsilon}-\sum_{k=1}^{2}\ell_{k}(4\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{1})q^{(1k)}(x/\varepsilon)\bigg)\,{\rm d}x.

(5.15)

Then by (
5.14
) and another use of (
5.9
) and (
5.13
), we have

\bigg|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{r,+}}\bigg(p^{\varepsilon}-\sum_{k=1}^{2}\ell_{k}(4r)q^{(1k)}(x/\varepsilon)\bigg)\,{\rm d}x-\bar{P}_{1}\bigg|\leq Cr^{\gamma}(M+M^{4+2\gamma+\delta}).

On the other hand, by Bogovskii’s lemma applied to the John domain between
B^{\varepsilon}_{r,+}
and
B^{\varepsilon}_{2r,+}
given by Definition
1.2
and (
5.10
)
with
4\delta
replaced by
\delta
, we have

\displaystyle\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{r,+}^{\varepsilon}}\bigg|p^{\varepsilon}-\sum_{k=1}^{2}\ell_{k}(4r)q^{(1k)}(x/\varepsilon)-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{r,+}}\bigg(p^{\varepsilon}-\sum_{k=1}^{2}\ell_{k}(4r)q^{(1k)}(x/\varepsilon)\bigg)\,{\rm d}x\bigg|^{2}\,{\rm d}x\bigg)^{1/2}

(5.16)

\displaystyle\leq C\bigg\{\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{2r,+}}\Big|\nabla u^{\varepsilon}-\nabla\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{2}\ell_{k}(4r)(P^{(1k)}+\varepsilon v^{(1k)}(x/\varepsilon)\Big)\Big|^{2}\,{\rm d}x\bigg)^{1/2}+\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{{8r},+}}|F^{\varepsilon}|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq CH_{\rm 1st}(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};4r)+C{\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q_{8r}}|\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\varepsilon}[F^{\varepsilon}]|^{3}\bigg)^{1/3}}

\displaystyle\leq Cr^{\gamma}(M+M^{4+2\gamma+\delta}).

Combining the above two inequalities, we obtain the desired estimate in (
1.3
) for the pressure. This completes the proof of Theorem
B
.

\Box

5.2.
Large-scale
C^{2,\gamma}
estimate over periodic boundaries

The goal of this subsection is to prove the large-scale
C^{2,\gamma}
regularity stated in Theorem
C
. In this subsection, we assume
\Omega
is a periodic bumpy John domain defined in Definition
1.3
. The argument for
C^{2,\gamma}
estimate is similar to the
C^{1,\gamma}
estimate. Throughout, we assume
(w_{1},\pi_{1})\in\mathscr{Q}_{1}(\Omega)
and
(w_{2},q_{2})\in\mathscr{Q}_{2}(\Omega)
. In other words, for some
\ell_{1k},\ell_{2j}\in\mathbb{R}
.

\displaystyle(w_{1},\pi_{1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{2}\ell_{1k}(P^{(1k)}+v^{(1k)},q^{(1k)}),

\displaystyle(w_{2},\pi_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{6}\ell_{2j}(P^{(2j)}+v^{(2j)},L^{(2j)}+q^{(2j)}).

It is important to observe that, by rescaling,

\big(\varepsilon w_{1}(x/\varepsilon)+\varepsilon^{2}w_{2}(x/\varepsilon),{\pi}_{1}(x/\varepsilon)+\varepsilon{\pi}_{2}(x/\varepsilon)\big)

is a solution of the Stokes system in
\Omega^{\varepsilon}
with the no-slip boundary condition on
\partial\Omega^{\varepsilon}
.

Define the second-order excess as

\displaystyle H_{\rm 2nd}(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};\rho)

\displaystyle=\inf\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}(w_{1},q_{1})\in\mathscr{Q}_{1}(\Omega)\\
(w_{2},q_{2})\in\mathscr{Q}_{2}(\Omega)\end{subarray}}\Bigg\{\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{\rho,+}}\big|\nabla u^{\varepsilon}-\nabla\big(\varepsilon w_{1}(x/\varepsilon)+\varepsilon^{2}w_{2}(x/\varepsilon)\big)\big|^{2}\,{\rm d}x\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle+\sup_{s,t\in[1/16,1/4]}\bigg|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{s\rho,+}}\big(p^{\varepsilon}-\pi_{1}(x/\varepsilon)-\varepsilon\pi_{2}(x/\varepsilon)\big)\,{\rm d}x-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{t\rho,+}}\big(p^{\varepsilon}-\pi_{1}(x/\varepsilon)-\varepsilon\pi_{2}(x/\varepsilon)\big)\,{\rm d}x\bigg|\bigg\}.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
L\in(0,\infty)
and
\Omega
be a bumpy periodic John domain with constant
L
according to Definition
1.3
.
Let
(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon})
be as in Theorem
C
, namely, a weak solution of (
Sε
) in Subsection
3.1
satisfying (
1.1
). For all
\varepsilon\in(0,{\frac{1}{32}}]
,
r\in[2\varepsilon,{\frac{1}{16}}]
and
\theta\in(0,{\frac{1}{8}}]
,

\displaystyle H_{{\rm 2nd}}(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};\theta r)

\displaystyle\leq C\big(\theta^{2}+\theta^{-3}\big(\frac{\varepsilon}{r}\big)^{1/12}\big)\Phi(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};16r)

(5.17)

\displaystyle+C\theta^{-3}\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{{Q_{10r}}}|\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\varepsilon}[F^{\varepsilon}]|^{3}\bigg)^{1/3},

where
C
depends only on
L
.

Proof.

The proof follows from the strategy developed in Section
3
, in particular from Lemma
3.2
to Lemma
3.7
. Let
(v_{r},q_{r})
be the solution of the approximate problem (
Sr
).
We will first use the
C^{2,1}
estimate of
v_{r}=(v_{r,1},v_{r,2},v_{r,3})
at the lower boundary
x_{3}=-\varepsilon
. Precisely, in view of no-slip Stokes polynomials defined in Section
4.1
, the
C^{2,1}
estimate
v_{r}
gives

\displaystyle\Big|v_{r}(x)-\sum_{k=1}^{2}\ell_{1k}^{*}P^{(1k)}(x+\varepsilon\mathbf{e}_{3})-\sum_{j=1}^{6}\ell_{2j}^{*}P^{(2j)}(x+\varepsilon\mathbf{e}_{3})\Big|

(5.18)

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{|x+\varepsilon\mathbf{e}_{3}|^{3}}{r^{2}}\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q_{r}^{\varepsilon}}|\nabla v_{r}|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2},

for
x\in Q_{r/2}^{\varepsilon}
, where we choose
\ell_{1k}^{*}=\frac{\partial v_{r,k}}{\partial x_{3}}(-\varepsilon\mathbf{e}_{3})
for
k=1,2
and
\ell_{21}^{*}=\frac{\partial^{2}v_{r,1}}{\partial x_{2}\partial x_{3}}(-\varepsilon\mathbf{e}_{3}),\ell_{22}^{*}={\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}v_{r,1}}{{\partial x_{3}^{2}}}(-\varepsilon\mathbf{e}_{3}),\ell_{23}^{*}=\frac{\partial^{2}v_{r,2}}{\partial x_{1}\partial x_{3}}(-\varepsilon\mathbf{e}_{3}),\ell_{24}^{*}={\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}v_{r,2}}{{\partial x_{3}^{2}}}(-\varepsilon\mathbf{e}_{3}),\ell_{25}^{*}={-\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}v_{r,1}}{\partial x_{1}\partial x_{3}}(-\varepsilon\mathbf{e}_{3}),\ell_{26}^{*}={-\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}v_{r,2}}{\partial x_{2}\partial x_{3}}(-\varepsilon\mathbf{e}_{3})
.

Moreover,

\sum_{k=1}^{2}|\ell_{1k}^{*}|+r\sum_{j=1}^{6}|\ell_{2j}^{*}|\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q_{r}^{\varepsilon}}|\nabla v_{r}|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}.

(5.19)

Observe that

\displaystyle v_{r}^{*}(x)

\displaystyle=v_{r}(x)-\sum_{k=1}^{2}\ell_{1k}^{*}P^{(1k)}(x+\varepsilon\mathbf{e}_{3})-\sum_{j=1}^{6}\ell_{2j}^{*}P^{(2j)}(x+\varepsilon\mathbf{e}_{3}),

\displaystyle q_{r}^{*}(x)

\displaystyle=q_{r}(x)-\sum_{j=1}^{6}\ell_{2j}^{*}L^{(2j)}(x+\varepsilon\mathbf{e}_{3}),

is a solution of the Stokes system in
Q_{r}^{\varepsilon}
with a no-slip condition on
x_{3}=-\varepsilon
.
Therefore, for any
\theta\in(0,\frac{1}{8}]
and
r>\varepsilon
,
it follows from (
5.18
) and the Caccioppoli inequality in rectangular region
Q_{2\theta r}^{\varepsilon}
that

\displaystyle\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q^{\varepsilon}_{\theta r}}|\nabla v_{r}^{*}|\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\theta r}\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q^{\varepsilon}_{2\theta r}}|v_{r}^{*}|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}

(5.20)

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(\theta^{2}+\theta^{-1}\big(\frac{\varepsilon}{r}\big)\Big)\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{{Q_{r}^{\varepsilon}}}|\nabla v_{r}|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}.

Then (
5.19
) implies

\displaystyle\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q^{\varepsilon}_{\theta r}}\Big|\nabla v_{r}-\nabla\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{2}\ell_{1k}^{*}P^{(1k)}+\sum_{j=1}^{6}\ell_{2j}^{*}P^{(2j)}\Big)\Big|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}

(5.21)

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(\theta^{2}+\theta^{-1}\big(\frac{\varepsilon}{r}\big)\Big)\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q_{r}^{\varepsilon}}|\nabla v_{r}|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}.

Next, to see the oscillation estimate for the pressure, applying Bogovskii’s lemma to
q_{r}^{*}
and the Caccioppoli inequality to
v_{r}^{*}
(combined with (
5.20
))
in Lipschitz domains, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{s,t\in[1/16,1/4]}\bigg|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q^{\varepsilon}_{s\theta r}}q_{r}^{*}-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q^{\varepsilon}_{t\theta r}}q_{r}^{*}\bigg|\leq C\Big(\theta^{2}+\theta^{-1}\big(\frac{\varepsilon}{r}\big)\Big)\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q_{r}^{\varepsilon}}|\nabla v_{r}|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}.

(5.22)

Notice that
L^{(2j)}
are linear functions. Thus, an application of (
5.19
) and the triangle inequality to (
5.22
) leads to

\displaystyle\sup_{s,t\in[1/16,1/4]}\bigg|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q^{\varepsilon}_{s\theta r}}\bigg(q_{r}-\sum_{j=1}^{6}\ell_{2j}^{*}L^{(2j)}\bigg)-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q^{\varepsilon}_{t\theta r}}\bigg(q_{r}-\sum_{j=1}^{6}\ell_{2j}^{*}L^{(2j)}\bigg)\bigg|

(5.23)

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(\theta^{2}+\theta^{-1}\big(\frac{\varepsilon}{r}\big)\Big)\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q_{r}^{\varepsilon}}|\nabla v_{r}|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}.

This, combined with (
5.21
), gives

\displaystyle\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q^{\varepsilon}_{\theta r}}\Big|\nabla v_{r}-\nabla\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{2}\ell_{1k}^{*}P^{(1k)}+\sum_{j=1}^{6}\ell_{2j}^{*}P^{(2j)}\Big)\Big|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle+\sup_{s,t\in[1/16,1/4]}\bigg|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q^{\varepsilon}_{s\theta r}}\bigg(q_{r}-\sum_{j=1}^{6}\ell_{2j}^{*}L^{(2j)}\bigg)-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q^{\varepsilon}_{t\theta r}}\bigg(q_{r}-\sum_{j=1}^{6}\ell_{2j}^{*}L^{(2j)}\bigg)\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(\theta^{2}+\theta^{-1}\big(\frac{\varepsilon}{r}\big)\Big)\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{Q_{r}^{\varepsilon}}|\nabla v_{r}|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}.

This is the key second-order excess estimate we need for
(v_{r},q_{r})
in
Q_{r}^{\varepsilon}
. To proceed, we follow the similar argument developed in Section
3
. Precisely, using an analogue of Lemma
3.4
and taking the approximation estimate in Lemma
3.2
, we can replace
(v_{r},q_{r})
by
(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon})
with new errors in
u^{\varepsilon}
and
F^{\varepsilon}
. Combined with the energy estimate for (
Sr
),
