we now have

\displaystyle\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{r,+}}\Big|\nabla u^{\varepsilon}-\nabla\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{2}\ell_{1k}^{*}P^{(1k)}+\sum_{j=1}^{6}\ell_{2j}^{*}P^{(2j)}\Big)\Big|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}

(5.24)

\displaystyle+\sup_{s,t\in[1/16,1/4]}\bigg|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{{B^{\varepsilon}_{s\theta r,+}}}\bigg(p^{\varepsilon}-\sum_{j=1}^{6}\ell_{2j}^{*}L^{(2j)}\bigg)-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{t\theta r,+}}\bigg(p^{\varepsilon}-\sum_{j=1}^{6}\ell_{2j}^{*}L^{(2j)}\bigg)\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(\theta^{2}+\theta^{-3}\big(\frac{\varepsilon}{r})^{1/12}\Big)\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{{B^{\varepsilon}_{5r,+}}}|\nabla u^{\varepsilon}|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}+C\theta^{-3}\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{{Q_{4r}}}|\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\varepsilon}[F^{\varepsilon}]|^{3}\bigg)^{1/3}.

Next, we insert the boundary layers into the above inequality. By (
5.8
) and

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{6}\bigg\{\frac{1}{r}\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{\theta r,+}}|\varepsilon(\nabla v^{(2j)})(x/\varepsilon)|^{2}\,{\rm d}x\bigg)^{1/2}+\frac{1}{r}\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{\theta r,+}}|\varepsilon q^{(2j)}(x/\varepsilon)|^{2}\,{\rm d}x\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq C\theta^{-1/2}\big(\frac{\varepsilon}{r}\big)^{1/2},

which follows from Proposition
4.7
,
we obtain from
(
5.24
) and (
5.19
)
along with the energy estimate for (
Sr
)
that

\displaystyle\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{\theta r,+}}\Big|\nabla u^{\varepsilon}-\nabla\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{2}\ell_{1k}^{*}(P^{(1k)}+\varepsilon v^{(1k)}(x/\varepsilon))

(5.25)

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{6}\ell_{2j}^{*}(P^{(2j)}+\varepsilon^{2}v^{(2j)}(x/\varepsilon))\Big)\Big|^{2}\,{\rm d}x\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle+\sup_{s,t\in[1/16,1/4]}\bigg|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{{B^{\varepsilon}_{s\theta r,+}}}\bigg(p^{\varepsilon}-\sum_{k=1}^{2}{\ell}^{*}_{1k}q^{(1k)}(x/\varepsilon)-\sum_{j=1}^{6}\ell_{2j}^{*}(L^{(2j)}+\varepsilon q^{(2j)}(x/\varepsilon))\bigg)\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{t\theta r,+}}\bigg(p^{\varepsilon}-\sum_{k=1}^{2}{\ell}^{*}_{1k}q^{(1k)}(x/\varepsilon)-\sum_{j=1}^{6}\ell_{2j}^{*}(L^{(2j)}+\varepsilon q^{(2j)}(x/\varepsilon))\bigg)\,{\rm d}x\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(\theta^{2}+\theta^{-3}\big(\frac{\varepsilon}{r})^{1/12}\Big)\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{{B^{\varepsilon}_{5r,+}}}|\nabla u^{\varepsilon}|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}+C\theta^{-3}\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{{Q_{4r}}}|\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\varepsilon}[F^{\varepsilon}]|^{3}\bigg)^{1/3}.

In view of the definition of
H_{\rm 2nd}
, we arrive at

\displaystyle H_{\rm 2nd}(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};\theta r)

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(\theta^{2}+\theta^{-3}\big(\frac{\varepsilon}{r})^{1/12}\Big)\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{{B^{\varepsilon}_{5r,+}}}|\nabla u^{\varepsilon}|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}+C\theta^{-3}\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{{Q_{4r}}}|\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\varepsilon}[F^{\varepsilon}]|^{3}\bigg)^{1/3},

which implies the desired estimate.
∎

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
L\in(0,\infty)
and
\Omega
be a bumpy periodic John domain with constant
L
according to Definition
1.3
. Let
(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon})
be as in Theorem
C
.
For any
\gamma\in[0,1)
,
\delta\in(0,1)
,
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]
and
r\in[\varepsilon,\frac{1}{2}]
,

\displaystyle H_{{\rm 2nd}}(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};r)\leq Cr^{1+\gamma}(M+M^{6+2\gamma+\delta}),

(5.26)

where
C
depends on
L
,
\gamma
and
\delta
. Here
M
is the number in Theorem
C
.

Proof.

For any
\gamma\in[0,1)
, we choose
an arbitrary
\delta>0
small enough so that
\delta<\frac{1-\gamma}{2}
. Then applying (
3.39
) with
\beta=1+\gamma+\delta
, we have

\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{{Q_{r}}}|\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\varepsilon}[F^{\varepsilon}]|^{3}\bigg)^{1/3}\leq C(M+M^{6+2\gamma+4\delta})r^{1+\gamma+\delta}.

Now, the rest of the proof is parallel to Proposition
5.3
. We omit the details.
∎

The following lemma is parallel to Lemma
5.4
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
L\in(0,\infty)
and
\Omega
be a bumpy John domain with constant
L
according to Definition
1.2
. For a given
\rho>0
, let

\ell_{1k}(\rho)
and
\ell_{2j}(\rho)
be the real numbers
so that

\displaystyle(w_{1},\pi_{1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{2}\ell_{1k}(\rho)(P^{(1k)}+v^{(1k)},q^{(1k)}),

\displaystyle(w_{2},\pi_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{6}\ell_{2j}(\rho)(P^{(2j)}+v^{(2j)},L^{(2j)}+q^{(2j)})

minimize
H_{{\rm 2nd}}(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};\rho)
. Then there exists a constant
\varepsilon_{2}\in(0,1)
so that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{2}]
and
r\in[\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{2},\frac{1}{2}]
,

\displaystyle\sup_{r_{1},r_{2}\in[r,2r]}\sum_{k=1}^{2}|\ell_{1k}(r_{1})-\ell_{1k}(r_{2})|

(5.27)

\displaystyle+\sup_{r_{1},r_{2}\in[r,2r]}\sum_{j=1}^{6}r|\ell_{2j}(r_{1})-\ell_{2j}(r_{2})|\leq C{\sup_{t\in[r,2r]}H_{{\rm 2nd}}(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};t),}

where
C
depends only on
L
.

Proof.

First, observe that for any
a_{k},b_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
,

\sum_{k=1}^{2}|a_{k}|+\sum_{j=1}^{6}|b_{j}|\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{1}(0)\cap\{x_{3}>0\}}\Big|\sum_{k=1}^{2}a_{k}P^{(1k)}+\sum_{j=1}^{6}b_{j}P^{(2j)}\Big|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}.

(5.28)

This inequality is true because
P^{(1k)}
and
P^{(2j)}
are all linearly independent polynomials. Recall that
P^{(1k)}
are homogeneous linear functions and
P^{(2j)}
are homogeneous quadratic functions. This means
P^{(1k)}(rx)=rP^{(1k)}(x)
and
P^{(2j)}(rx)=r^{2}P^{(2j)}(x)
. Fix
r_{1},r_{2}\in[r,2r]
. Applying (
5.28
) with
a_{k}=\ell_{1k}(r_{1})-\ell(r_{2})
and
b_{j}=r(\ell_{2j}(r_{1})-\ell_{2j}(r_{2}))
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{2}|\ell_{1k}(r_{1})-\ell_{1k}(r_{2})|+\sum_{j=1}^{6}r|\ell_{2j}(r_{1})-\ell_{2j}(r_{2})|

\displaystyle\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{1}(0)\cap\{x_{3}>0\}}\Big|\sum_{k=1}^{2}(\ell_{1k}(r_{1})-\ell_{1k}(r_{2}))P^{(1k)}+\sum_{j=1}^{6}r(\ell_{2j}(r_{1})-\ell_{2j}(r_{2}))P^{(2j)}\Big|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{r}\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{r}(0)\cap\{x_{3}>0\}}\Big|\sum_{k=1}^{2}(\ell_{1k}(r_{1})-\ell_{1k}(r_{2}))P^{(1k)}+\sum_{j=1}^{6}(\ell_{2j}(r_{1})-\ell_{2j}(r_{2}))P^{(2j)}\Big|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{r}(0)\cap\{x_{3}>0\}}\Big|\sum_{k=1}^{2}(\ell_{1k}(r_{1})-\ell_{1k}(r_{2}))\nabla P^{(1k)}+\sum_{j=1}^{6}(\ell_{2j}(r_{1})-\ell_{2j}(r_{2}))\nabla P^{(2j)}\Big|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2},

where the Poincaré inequality has been applied in the last line.
Now, inserting
u^{\varepsilon},v^{(1k)}(x/\varepsilon)
and
v^{(2j)}(x/\varepsilon)
into the right-hand side, and using the triangle inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{2}|\ell_{1k}(r_{1})-\ell_{1k}(r_{2})|+\sum_{j=1}^{6}r|\ell_{2j}(r_{1})-\ell_{2j}(r_{2})|

\displaystyle\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{r,+}^{\varepsilon}}\Big|\nabla\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{2}(\ell_{1k}(r_{1})-\ell_{1k}(r_{2}))(P^{(1k)}+\varepsilon v^{(1k)}(x/\varepsilon))

\displaystyle+{\sum_{j=1}^{6}}(\ell_{2j}(r_{1})-\ell_{2j}(r_{2}))(P^{(2j)}+\varepsilon^{2}v^{(2j)}(x/\varepsilon))\Big)\Big|^{2}\,{\rm d}x\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle+C\sum_{k=1}^{2}|\ell_{1k}(r_{1})-\ell_{1k}(r_{2})|\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{r,+}^{\varepsilon}}|(\nabla v^{(1j)})(x/\varepsilon)|^{2}\,{\rm d}x\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle+\frac{C}{r}\sum_{j=1}^{6}r|\ell_{2j}(r_{1})-\ell_{2j}(r_{2})|\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{r,+}^{\varepsilon}}|\varepsilon(\nabla v^{(2j)})(x/\varepsilon)|^{2}\,{\rm d}x\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq CH_{\rm 2nd}(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};r_{1})+CH_{\rm 2nd}(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon};r_{2})

\displaystyle+C_{1}\big(\frac{\varepsilon}{r}\big)^{1/2}\sum_{j=1}^{2}|\ell_{1k}(r_{1})-\ell_{1k}(r_{2})|+C_{2}\big(\frac{\varepsilon}{r}\big)^{1/2}\sum_{k=1}^{6}r|\ell_{2j}(r_{1})-\ell_{2j}(r_{2})|,

where Proposition
4.7
is applied in the last inequality.
Thus, if
r>\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{2}
for some sufficiently small constant
\varepsilon_{2}\in(0,1)
so that
C_{1}(\varepsilon/r)^{1/2}<\frac{1}{2}
and
C_{2}(\varepsilon/r)^{1/2}<\frac{1}{2}
, then

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{2}|\ell_{1k}(r_{1})-\ell_{1k}(r_{2})|+\sum_{j=1}^{6}r|\ell_{2j}(r_{1})-\ell_{2j}(r_{2})|

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{i=1}^{2}H_{\rm 2nd}(w^{\varepsilon},\pi^{\varepsilon};r_{i}).

This leads to the assertion.
∎

Proof of Theorem
C
:

The estimate for the velocity is contained in (
5.26
). The estimate for pressure can be derived similarly as Theorem
B
. The details are left to the reader.

\Box

5.3.
Liouville-type results

As an application of the construction of boundary layers and uniform regularity, a Liouville-type theorem for Stokes systems can be shown by the large-scale Lipschitz,
C^{1,\gamma}
and
C^{2,\gamma}
estimates. We point out that our large-scale regularity results hold also for the linear Stokes system, although with linear dependence on
M
in the right-hand sides of (
1.2
), (
1.3
) and (
1.4
). The proofs are simpler, using that the source term
F^{\varepsilon}=0
. To describe the Liouville-type theorem, consider the Stokes system in the entire
\Omega

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\Delta u+\nabla p=0,&x\in\Omega\\
\nabla\cdot u=0,&x\in\Omega\\
u=0,&x\in\partial\Omega,\end{array}\right.

(5.29)

where
\Omega
is a bumpy John domain according to Definition
1.2
. Let
B_{R}=B_{R}(0)
. We state the Liouville-type theorem as follows. Its proof follows from a routine rescaling of the large-scale regularity estimates. Notice that this result complements Corollary
3.1
already stated above.

Theorem 5.8
.

Let
\Omega
be a bumpy John domain according to Definition
1.2
. Let
(u,p)
be a weak solution of (
5.29
).

(i)

If for some
\sigma\in(0,1)

\liminf_{R\to\infty}\frac{1}{R^{1+\sigma}}\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{R,+}}|u|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}=0,

then
(u,p)\in\mathscr{Q}_{1}(\Omega)
(up to a constant for
p
).

(ii)

In addition, assume
\Omega
is periodic bumpy John domain according to Definition
1.3
. If for some
\sigma\in(0,1)
,

\liminf_{R\to\infty}\frac{1}{R^{2+\sigma}}\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{R,+}}|u|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}=0,

then
(u,p)\in\mathscr{Q}_{1}(\Omega)+\mathscr{Q}_{2}(\Omega)
(up to a constant for
p
).

Appendix A
Bogovskii’s lemma and some applications

For a bounded open set
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
p\in(1,\infty)
, let

\displaystyle L^{q}_{0}(D)=\bigg\{f\in L^{q}(D)~\bigg|~\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{D}f=0\bigg\}.

Theorem A.1

(
[
2
, Theorem 4.1]
)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a bounded John domain according to Definition
1.1
with constant
L
. There exists an operator
\mathbb{B}:L^{q}_{0}(\Omega)\to W^{1,q}_{0}(\Omega)^{3}
satisfying

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\mathbb{B}[f]=f\hskip 10.00002pt{\rm in}\ \,\Omega

and

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{B}[f]\|_{W^{1,q}(\Omega)}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)},

(A.1)

with
C
depending on
L
.

Lemma A.2
.

Let
\Omega
be a bounded John domain according to Definition
1.1
. Set

\displaystyle H^{1}_{0,\sigma}(\Omega):=\{u\in{H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)^{3}}~|~\nabla\cdot u=0\ \,{\rm in}\ \Omega\}.

Let
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)^{3}
and
F\in L^{2}(\Omega)^{3\times 3}
. If
u\in H^{1}(\Omega)^{3}
is a weak solution of the Stokes equations in the sense

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\varphi=\int_{\Omega}f\cdot\varphi-\int_{\Omega}F\cdot\nabla\varphi,\hskip 10.00002pt\varphi\in{H^{1}_{0,\sigma}(\Omega)},

then there exists a function
p\in L^{2}(\Omega)
unique up to a constant for which we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\phi-\int_{\Omega}p(\nabla\cdot\phi)=\int_{\Omega}f\cdot\phi-\int_{\Omega}F\cdot\nabla\phi,\hskip 10.00002pt\phi\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)^{3}.

Namely, the pair
(u,p)
is a weak solution of the Stokes equations. Moreover,

\|p-{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega}}p\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\Big(\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+{\rm diam}(\Omega)\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|F\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\Big),

(A.2)

where
{\rm diam}(\Omega)
denotes the diameter of
\Omega
.

A direct application of Bogovskii’s operator is the Caccioppoli inequality for the Stokes equations. Let
Q_{r,+}=Q_{r}\cap\{x_{3}>0\}
. Suppose
(u,p)
is a weak solution of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\Delta u+\nabla p=\nabla\cdot F&\mbox{in}\ Q_{2r,+}\\
\nabla\cdot u=0&\mbox{in}\ Q_{2r,+}\\
u=0&\mbox{on}\ Q_{2r}\cap\{x_{3}=0\}.\end{array}\right.

(A.3)

The following is the Caccioppoli inequality over flat boundaries whose proof is classical
[
45
, Theorem 1.1]
(the interior Caccioppoli inequality is similar).

Lemma A.3
.

Let
F\in L^{2}(Q_{2r,+})^{3\times 3}

and let
(u,p)\in H^{1}(Q_{2r,+})^{3}\times L^{2}(Q_{2r,+})
be a weak solution to (
A.3
).
Then,

\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(Q_{r,+})}\leq C\bigg(\frac{1}{r}\|u\|_{L^{2}(Q_{2r,+})}+\|F\|_{L^{2}(Q_{2r,+})}\bigg),

(A.4)

where the constant
C
is independent of
r
.

Now, consider the Stokes equations over John boundaries

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\Delta u^{\varepsilon}+\nabla p^{\varepsilon}=\nabla\cdot F^{\varepsilon}&\mbox{in}\ B^{\varepsilon}_{4r,+}\\
\nabla\cdot u^{\varepsilon}=0&\mbox{in}\ B^{\varepsilon}_{4r,+}\\
u^{\varepsilon}=0&\mbox{on}\ \Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{4r}.\end{array}\right.

(A.5)

Unfortunately, the Caccioppoli inequality in the form of (
A.4
) cannot be derived for the weak solution of (
A.5
) by the usual iteration argument (see e.g.,
[
45
, Lemma 0.5]
or
[
43
, Chapter V, Lemma 3.1]
) due to the assumption that the John domain condition (after rescaling) holds only for scales
r\geq\varepsilon
. Actually, we only have a weaker Caccioppoli inequality valid for
r\geq\varepsilon
, which is sufficient for us to show a (large-scale) Meyers estimate.

Lemma A.4

(A weak Caccioppoli inequality)
.

Let
L\in(0,\infty)
and
\Omega
be a bumpy John domain with constant
L
according to Definition
1.2
. Let
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]
and
F^{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}(B^{\varepsilon}_{4r,+})^{3\times 3}
, and let
(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon})\in H^{1}(B^{\varepsilon}_{4r,+})^{3}\times L^{2}(B_{4r,+}^{\varepsilon})
be a weak solution to (
A.5
) with
r\geq\varepsilon
.
Then for any
\theta\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\nabla u^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(B^{\varepsilon}_{{r},+})}\leq\theta\|\nabla u^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(B^{\varepsilon}_{{4r},+})}+\frac{C}{\theta r}\|u^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(B^{\varepsilon}_{4r,+})}+C\|F^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(B^{\varepsilon}_{4r,+})}\,,\end{split}

(A.6)

where the constant
C
depends only on
L
. In particular
C
is independent of

\theta,\varepsilon
and
r
. Moreover, if
r\geq 4\varepsilon
, then by the standard interior Caccioppoli inequality and a covering argument as in the proof of Lemma
2.2
,
B^{\varepsilon}_{4r,+}
may be replaced by
B^{\varepsilon}_{2r,+}
on the right-hand side of (
A.6
).

Proof.

Let
\phi_{r}
be a smooth cut-off function so that
\phi_{r}(x)=1
for
{x}\in B_{r}
,
\phi(x)=0
for
x\notin B_{2r}
and
|\nabla\phi|\leq C/r
. Integrating the first equation of (
A.5
) against
u^{\varepsilon}\phi^{2}
, we have

\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{2r,+}}\nabla u^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla u^{\varepsilon}\phi^{2}=-\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{2r,+}}\phi\nabla u^{\varepsilon}\cdot(\nabla\phi\otimes u^{\varepsilon})+\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{2r,+}}\nabla p^{\varepsilon}\cdot u^{\varepsilon}\phi^{2}-\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{2r,+}}F^{\cdot}\nabla(u^{\varepsilon}\phi^{2}).

(A.7)

The first and third terms on the right-hand side are routine.
To deal with the pressure, by Definition
1.2
of the bumpy John domain
\Omega
, we use the Bogovskii operator in a John domain
\Omega_{2r}^{\varepsilon}
satisfying
B_{2r,+}^{\varepsilon}\subset\Omega_{2r}^{\varepsilon}\subset B_{4r,+}^{\varepsilon}
and (
A.2
) to obtain

\bigg(\int_{\Omega_{2r}^{\varepsilon}}|p^{\varepsilon}-{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{2r}^{\varepsilon}}}p^{\varepsilon}|^{2}\bigg)\leq C\Big(\|\nabla u^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{2r}^{\varepsilon})}+\|F^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{2r}^{\varepsilon})}\Big).

Let
L=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{2r}^{\varepsilon}}p^{\varepsilon}
. Then, using the above estimate and
\nabla\cdot u^{\varepsilon}=0
,

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{2r,+}}\nabla p^{\varepsilon}\cdot u^{\varepsilon}\phi^{2}\bigg|

\displaystyle=\bigg|\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{2r,+}}\nabla(p^{\varepsilon}-L)\cdot u^{\varepsilon}\phi^{2}\bigg|

\displaystyle=\bigg|\int_{B^{\varepsilon}_{2r,+}}(p^{\varepsilon}-L)u^{\varepsilon}\cdot 2\phi\nabla\phi\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{r}\Big(\|\nabla u^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{2r}^{\varepsilon})}+\|F^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{2r}^{\varepsilon})}\Big)\|u^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(B_{2r,+}^{\varepsilon})}

\displaystyle\leq\theta^{2}\|\nabla u^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(B_{4r,+}^{\varepsilon})}^{2}+\frac{C}{\theta^{2}r^{2}}\|u^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(B_{4r,+}^{\varepsilon})}^{2}+C\|F^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(B^{\varepsilon}_{4r,+})},

for any
\theta\in(0,1)
. In view of (
A.7
), this gives the desired estimate by a standard argument.
∎

Appendix B
Large-scale estimates for the Green function

This appendix is devoted to the study of the Green function for the Stokes equations in a bumpy John half-space according to Definition
1.2
. The large-scale estimates proved in Section
3
will be applied. The basic scheme is to derive estimates for the velocity part of the Green function directly from the interior and large-scale boundary Lipschitz estimates. For this we follow the strategy pioneered in
[
9
,
10
]
. Then, we deduce the estimates for the pressure part of the Green function from Bogovskii’s lemma and the estimates for the velocity part.

We use
B_{R}(x)=Q_{R}(x)
to denote the cube centered at
x
with side length
2R
. If the center is not important in the context, it is abbreviated as
B_{R}
. Throughout this appendix,
\Omega_{\leq N}
,
\Omega_{\geq N}
,
\Omega_{<N}
, and
\Omega_{>N}
defined around (
4.7
) will be used. Moreover, let
\hat{x}
denote the projection of
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
on
\partial\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}
.

B.1.
Construction of the Green function

Let
D
be an open set in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Denote by
Y^{1,2}(D)
the space of functions

\{u\in L^{6}(D)~|~\nabla u\in L^{2}(D)^{3}\}

(B.1)

equipped with the norm
\|u\|_{Y^{1,2}(D)}=\|u\|_{L^{6}(D)}+\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(D)}
. Let
Y^{1,2}_{0}(D)
be the closure of
C_{0}^{\infty}(D)
under
\|\cdot\|_{Y^{1,2}(D)}
. The closed subspace of
Y^{1,2}_{0}(D)^{3}

\{u\in Y^{1,2}_{0}(D)^{3}~|~\nabla\cdot u=0\ \,{\rm in}\ D\}

(B.2)

is denoted by
Y^{1,2}_{0,\sigma}(D)
. Note that, when the Lebesgue measure of
D
is finite, we have
Y^{1,2}_{0}(D)=H^{1,2}_{0}(D)
by the Sobolev inequality
\|u\|_{L^{6}(D)}\leq C\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(D)}
if
u\in C_{0}^{\infty}(D)
. Moreover, we see that
Y^{1,2}_{0,\sigma}(D)
as well as
Y^{1,2}_{0}(D)^{3}
is a Hilbert space with an inner product
\langle u,v\rangle=\int_{D}\nabla u\cdot\nabla v
.

Let
\Omega
be a bumpy John domain with constant
L\in(0,\infty)
according to the Definition
1.2
. Based on similar proofs in
[
54
]
and
[
25
]
and using the large-scale Lipschitz estimate of Theorem
A
proved in Section
3
, we can construct the Green function
(G,\Pi)=(G(x,y),\Pi(x,y))
, which satisfies the following properties:

(i)

For any
q\in[1,\frac{3}{2})
,
G(\cdot,y)\in W^{1,q}_{0,{\rm loc}}(\overline{\Omega})^{3\times 3}

and
G(\cdot,y)\in Y^{1,2}(\Omega\setminus B_{r}(y))^{3\times 3}
for each
y\in\Omega
and
r>0
. Moreover,
\Pi(\cdot,y)\in L^{2}_{{\rm loc}}(\overline{\Omega\setminus B_{r}(y)})^{3\times 1}
for each
y\in\Omega
and
r>0
.

(ii)

(G(\cdot,y),\Pi(\cdot,y))
satisfies, for each
y\in\Omega
,

\int_{\Omega}\nabla G(\cdot,y)\cdot\nabla\phi-\int_{\Omega}\Pi(\cdot,y)(\nabla\cdot\phi)=\phi(y),\hskip 10.00002pt\phi\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\Omega)^{3}.

(B.3)

(iii)

For all
f\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)^{3}
, if
the function
(u,p)\in Y^{1,2}_{0,\sigma}(\Omega)^{3}\times L^{2}_{\rm loc}(\overline{\Omega})
, with
p(x)\to 0
as
x_{3}\to\infty
, satisfies the Stokes equations in the sense

\int_{\Omega}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\phi-\int_{\Omega}p(\nabla\cdot\phi)=\int_{\Omega}f\cdot\phi,\hskip 10.00002pt\phi\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\Omega)^{3},

(B.4)

then

u(x)=\int_{\Omega}G(x,y)f(y)\,{\rm d}y,\hskip 20.00003ptp(x)=\int_{\Omega}\Pi(x,y)\cdot f(y)\,{\rm d}y.

(B.5)

We describe how to obtain
(G,\Pi)
meeting Properties (i)-(iii) above. The existence and basic estimates of the velocity component
G(x,y)
follow from a similar argument as
[
54
, Theorem 4.1]
by working in the Hilbert space
Y^{1,2}_{0,\sigma}(\Omega)
. In fact, there is
G(x,y)
such that
u(x)
defined in (
B.5
) belongs to
u\in Y^{1,2}_{0,\sigma}(\Omega)
and is the unique solution of the Stokes equations in the sense

\int_{\Omega}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\varphi=\int_{\Omega}f\cdot\varphi,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for any }\varphi\in Y^{1,2}_{0,\sigma}(\Omega).

(B.6)

Then, by using Lemma
A.2
on each bounded John subdomain, one sees that there is a pressure
p\in L^{2}_{{\rm loc}}(\overline{\Omega})
for which we have (
B.4
), uniquely determined under the condition
p(x)\to 0
as
x_{3}\to\infty
.

When constructing the pressure component
\Pi(x,y)
in (
B.5
), we need a careful analysis since the domain is unbounded unlike in
[
25
]
. Here the oscillation estimate of
p
will play a crucial role. For an open set
E
, define the oscillation of
p
in
E
by

\osc_{E}p=\sup_{x,y\in E}|p(x)-p(y)|.

(B.7)

The following lemma shows a fundamental oscillation estimate for the pressure.

Lemma B.1
.

Let
L\in(0,\infty)
and
\Omega
be a bumpy John domain with constant
L
according to Definition
1.2
. Then we have the following statements.

(i)

Let
\overline{B_{R}}\subset\Omega
. If
-\Delta u+\nabla p=0
and
\nabla\cdot u=0
in
B_{R}
, then

\osc_{B_{R/2}}p\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{R}}|\nabla u|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2},

(B.8)

where
C
is a universal constant.

(ii)

Let
z\in\partial\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}
and let
R>2
. If
-\Delta u+\nabla p=0
and
\nabla\cdot u=0
in
\Omega\cap B_{R}(z)
and
u=0
on
\partial\Omega\cap B_{R}(z)
, then

\osc_{\Omega_{>2}\cap B_{R/2}(z)}p\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega\cap B_{R}(z)}|\nabla u|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2},

(B.9)

where
C
depends on
L
and is independent of
z
and
R
.

Proof.

The interior case (i) is classical and the proof is omitted. Let us prove the boundary case (ii). Since only the case where
R
is sufficiently large is nontrivial, we assume that
R>32
. For any
x\in\Omega_{>2}\cap B_{R/2}(z)
, the mean value property of harmonic functions yields

p(x)=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\mathcal{B}_{r}(x)}p,\hskip 20.00003pt\mbox{if}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathcal{B}_{r}(x):=\{y|\ |y-x|<r\}\subset\Omega.

(B.10)

Here we assume
r=\frac{x_{3}}{2}\leq\frac{R}{16}
, hence
r>1
. Note that if
x_{3}>\frac{R}{8}
, the oscillation can be handled by the interior estimate (
B.8
).

Recall that
\hat{x}
is the projection of
x
on
\partial\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}
.
Let
\Omega_{4r}(\hat{x})
be a John domain given by Definition
1.2
so that
\Omega\cap B_{4r}(\hat{x})\subset\Omega_{4r}(\hat{x})\subset\Omega\cap B_{8r}(\hat{x})
.
Clearly
\mathcal{B}_{r}(x)\subset\Omega_{4r}(\hat{x})
,

B_{R/4}(\hat{x})\subset B_{3R/4}(z)
and
B_{R/2}(\hat{x})\subset B_{R}(z)
.
By (
B.10
) and the Bogovskii lemma,

\displaystyle\bigg|p(x)-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{4r}(\hat{x})}p\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\mathcal{B}_{r}(x)}|p-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{4r}(\hat{x})}p|\leq C\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{{\Omega}_{4r}(\hat{x})}|p-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{4r}(\hat{x})}p|

(B.11)

\displaystyle\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{8r}(\hat{x})\cap\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{R/2}(\hat{x})\cap\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{R}(z)\cap\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2},

where we also used the Lipschitz estimate of
u
in the fourth inequality. Similarly, we have

\bigg|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{4r}(\hat{x})\cap\Omega}p-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{4r}(\hat{x})}p\bigg|\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{R}(z)\cap\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}.

On the other hand, by the pressure estimate for the Stokes system (an analogue of Theorem
A
with linear dependence on
M
),

\bigg|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{4r}(\hat{x})\cap\Omega}p-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{R/4}(\hat{x})\cap\Omega}p\bigg|\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{R}(z)\cap\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}.

Similar to (
B.11
), because
B_{R/4}(\hat{x})\subset B_{3R/4}(z)
, we obtain

\bigg|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{R/4}(\hat{x})\cap\Omega}p-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{3R/4}(z)\cap\Omega}p\bigg|\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{2R}(z)\cap\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}.

Finally, combining the above estimates, we arrive at

|p(x)-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{3R/4}(z)\cap\Omega}p|\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{2R}(z)\cap\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}.

(B.12)

Since
x\in\Omega_{>2}\cap B_{R/2}(z)
is arbitrary, this implies the estimate (
B.9
) with
2R
in the right-hand side instead of
R
. Then a covering argument using (
B.8
) yields the desired estimate (
B.9
). The proof is complete.
∎

Remark B.2
.

The interior oscillation estimate holds also for
-\Delta u+\nabla p=f
and
\nabla\cdot u=0
in
B_{R}
provided
f\in L^{q}(B_{R})^{3}
for some
q>3
. Precisely, by the classical Schauder theory,

\osc_{B_{R/2}}p\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{R}}|\nabla u|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}+CR\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{R}}|f|^{q}\bigg)^{1/q},

where
C
is a universal constant.

Now, we are ready to construct
\Pi(x,y)
and prove Properties (i)-(iii) of
(G,\Pi)
. For a given
f\in C_{0}^{\infty}(B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega)^{3}
with
R>32
, we consider the Stokes equations (
B.4
) with
u
given by (
B.5
) and the associated pressure
p\in L^{2}_{{\rm loc}}(\overline{\Omega})
.
For
x\in\Omega_{>2}
such that
|x|\geq 4R
, we set
r=\frac{|x|}{2}\geq 2R
.
Since
f
is supported in
B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega
, we have
-\Delta u+\nabla p=0
in
B_{r}(x)\cap\Omega
. Moreover, by an energy estimate using (
B.6
), we have

\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{r}(x)\cap\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\leq\frac{CR}{r^{3/2}}\|{f}\|_{L^{2}(B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega)}.

(B.13)

Therefore, Lemma
B.1
and a covering argument imply the oscillation estimate of
p
, namely,

\osc_{\Omega_{>2}\cap B_{2r}(0)\setminus\overline{B_{r}(0)}}p\leq\frac{CR}{r^{3/2}}\|{f}\|_{L^{2}(B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega)}.

This further implies

\displaystyle\osc_{\Omega_{>2}\setminus\overline{B_{r}(0)}}p

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\osc_{\Omega_{>2}\cap B_{2^{k}r}(0)\setminus\overline{B_{2^{k-1}r}(0)}}p

(B.14)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{CR}{{(2^{k-1}r)}^{3/2}}\|{f}\|_{L^{2}(B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{CR}{r^{3/2}}\|{f}\|_{L^{2}(B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega)}.

This shows that
p(x)
converges to a constant as
x\to\infty
. By the assumption that
p(x)\to 0
as
x_{3}\to\infty
, we know the limiting constant is zero. Hence, in view of (
B.14
), we derive

|p(x)|\leq\frac{CR}{|x|^{3/2}}\|{f}\|_{L^{2}(B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega)},

(B.15)

for all
x\in\Omega_{>2}
satisfying
|x|\geq 4R
. Moreover, by arguing in a similar manner as in Step 3 in the proof of Theorem
4.1
and using (
B.15
) instead of (
4.15
), we find that for sufficiently large
R^{\prime}\geq R
,

\displaystyle\|p\|_{L^{2}(B_{R^{\prime}}(0)\cap\Omega)}\leq C(R^{\prime})\|f\|_{L^{2}(B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega)}

(B.16)

with a constant
C(R^{\prime})
depending on
R^{\prime}
.

On the other hand, for
x
with either
x\in\Omega_{\leq 2}
or
|x|\geq 4R
, we can connect
x
to another point
\tilde{x}\in\Omega_{>2}
with
|\tilde{x}|\geq 4R
by a chain of a finite number of cubes
\{B_{r_{i}}(z_{i})~|~i=1,2,\cdots,N\}
such that
B_{2r_{i}}(z_{i})\subset\Omega
. Using Remark
B.2
on each
B_{r_{i}}(z_{i})
, as well as (
B.15
) applied to
\tilde{x}
, we see that for any
x,R

|p(x)|\leq C_{q}(x,R)\|{f}\|_{L^{q}(B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega)}

(B.17)

provided
q>3
, where
C_{q}(x,R)
is a constant depending only on
q,x
, and
R
.

From (
B.15
) and (
B.17
), for each fixed
x\in\Omega
, the map
f\mapsto p(x)
is a bounded linear functional on
L^{q}(B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega)^{3}
. By the Riesz representation theorem, there is a unique function
\Pi(x,\cdot)\in L^{q^{\prime}}(B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega)^{3}
with
q^{\prime}\in[1,\frac{3}{2})
, so that

p(x)=\int_{B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega}\Pi(x,y)\cdot f(y)\,{\rm d}y.

Note that the above
\Pi(x,\cdot)
is only defined in
B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega
for a fixed
x
. As
x
and
R
vary, we can obtain a family of such functions, which can be glued together by the uniqueness of
p
. Thus we have constructed a function
\Pi(x,y)
defined in the entire
\Omega\times\Omega
satisfying
\Pi(x,\cdot)\in L^{q^{\prime}}_{\rm loc}(\overline{\Omega})^{3}
. To investigate the local integrability of
\Pi(\cdot,\cdot)
, let us fix
R>1
and define a functional
S(f,g)
for smooth
f,g
supported in
B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega
by

\displaystyle S(f,g)

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega}p(x)g(x)\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega}\int_{B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega}\big(\Pi(x,y)\cdot f(y)\big)g(x)\,{\rm d}y\,{\rm d}x.

From (
B.16
), by taking a sufficiently large
R^{\prime}\geq R
,
we see that

\displaystyle|S(f,g)|

\displaystyle\leq\|p\|_{L^{2}(B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq{C(R^{\prime})}\|f\|_{L^{2}(B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega)}.

Hence
S
is a bounded functional on
L^{2}(B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega)^{3}\times L^{2}(B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega)
, which implies that

\int_{B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega}\Pi(x,\cdot)g(x)\,{\rm d}x\hskip 10.00002pt\text{is in}\hskip 10.00002ptL^{2}(B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega)^{3}.

(B.18)

Now we can prove that
(G,\Pi)
satisfies Properties (i)-(iii). Property (iii) is obvious from the arguments so far. Property (ii) follows from Property (iii) combined with the Lebesgue differentiation theorem. Here we use the fact that, for all
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)^{3}
, the function of
y

\int_{\Omega}\Pi(x,y)(\nabla\cdot\phi)(x)\,{\rm d}x

belongs to
L^{2}_{{\rm loc}}(\overline{\Omega})^{3}
because of (
B.18
). The integrability of
\Pi(\cdot,y)
in Property (i) follows from the weak form (
B.3
).
Consequently, we have constructed the Green function
(G,\Pi)
meeting Properties (i)-(iii).

We should point out that in the above argument for existence, the estimate, for example of
\Pi(x,\cdot)
, is very rough, especially when
x
is close to the boundary
\partial\Omega
. This is because the large-scale regularity of
\Pi(x,\cdot)
is not taken into consideration. In the following, we obtain some more careful estimates of
(G,\Pi)
by studying the equation (
B.3
).

B.2.
Large-scale estimates of the velocity component

For convenience, let
G(x,y)
and
\Pi(x,y)
be zero-extended for both
x
and
y
. Recall the symmetry
G(x,y)=G^{t}(y,x)
, where
G^{t}
is the transpose of
G
.
Thus by definition,
G(x,y)=0
if either
x\in\partial\Omega
or
y\in\partial\Omega
and
x\neq y
.
Denote by
\delta(x)
the distance from
x
to
\partial\Omega
.

Notice that
\nabla_{x}G
denotes the derivative of
G
with respect to the first variable, i.e.

(\nabla_{x}G)(x,y)=(\nabla G(\cdot,y))(x),\hskip 20.00003pt\mbox{for all}\hskip 10.00002pt(x,y)\in\Omega.

Similarly,
\nabla_{y}G
denotes the derivative of
G
with respect to the second variable. The following estimates for the derivatives of
G
are crucial.

Proposition B.3
.

Let
L\in(0,\infty)
and
\Omega
be a bumpy John domain with constant
L
according to Definition
1.2
. The velocity component
G(x,y)
satisfies
:

(i)

For
x_{3}>2
and
y_{3}>2
,

|\nabla_{x}G(x,y)|\leq C\min\bigg\{\frac{1}{|x-y|^{2}},\frac{\delta(y)}{|x-y|^{3}}\bigg\},

(B.19)

and

|\nabla_{y}G(x,y)|\leq C\min\bigg\{\frac{1}{|x-y|^{2}},\frac{\delta(x)}{|x-y|^{3}}\bigg\}.

(B.20)

(ii)

For
x_{3}>2
and
y_{3}<2
with
|x-y|>{32}
,

\bigg(\int_{B_{1}(y)}|\nabla_{y}G(x,z)|^{2}\,{\rm d}z\bigg)^{1/2}\leq C\min\bigg\{\frac{1}{|x-y|^{2}},\frac{\delta(x)}{|x-y|^{3}}\bigg\},

(B.21)

and

\bigg(\int_{B_{1}(y)}|\nabla_{x}G(x,z)|^{2}\,{\rm d}z\bigg)^{1/2}\leq\frac{C}{|x-y|^{3}}.

(B.22)

(iii)

For
x_{3}<2
and
y_{3}>2
with
|x-y|>{32}
,

\bigg(\int_{B_{1}(x)}|\nabla_{x}G(z,y)|^{2}\,{\rm d}z\bigg)^{1/2}\leq C\min\bigg\{\frac{1}{|x-y|^{2}},\frac{\delta(y)}{|x-y|^{3}}\bigg\},

(B.23)

and

\bigg(\int_{B_{1}(x)}|\nabla_{y}G(z,y)|^{2}\,{\rm d}z\bigg)^{1/2}\leq\frac{C}{|x-y|^{3}}.

(B.24)

Here
C
depends on
L
.

Notice that
G
and
\Pi
are zero-extended outside
\Omega
. Therefore, the integrals above make sense even in the case when
B_{1}(x)
or
B_{1}(y)
intersect
\Omega^{{\rm c}}
. For the estimates concerned with the oscillation of the pressure, on the contrary, we make precise when the balls intersect the boundary; see for instance Lemma
B.1
.

Proof of Proposition
B.3
.

Note that (ii) and (iii) are symmetric. While (i) is the interior estimate whose proof is similar to (ii) and (iii).
Hence, we will only prove (ii). Since we are working on cubes, it is more convenient to define
R=|x-y|_{\infty}:=\max_{1\leq i\leq 3}|x_{i}-y_{i}|
, which is comparable to the usual distance
|x-y|
. Recall that
(G(x,\cdot),\Pi(x,\cdot))
is a weak solution of Stoke system in
\Omega\setminus\{x\}
. To show (
B.21
), we begin with the interior and boundary Lipschitz estimates for
G(x,\cdot)
,

\displaystyle\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{1}(y)}|\nabla_{y}G(x,z)|^{2}{\,{\rm d}z}\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{3}(\hat{y})}|\nabla_{y}G(x,z)|^{2}{\,{\rm d}z}\bigg)^{1/2}

(B.25)

\displaystyle\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{R/2}(\hat{y})}|\nabla_{y}G(x,z)|^{2}{\,{\rm d}z}\bigg)^{1/2}.

where
\hat{y}
is the projection of
y
on
\{y_{3}=0\}
.

To proceed, let
F\in L^{2}(B_{R/2}(\hat{y})\cap\Omega)^{3\times 3}
(zero-extended to the whole of
\Omega
). Let
(u,p)
be the weak solution of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\Delta u+\nabla p=\nabla\cdot F&\text{in }\Omega\\
\nabla\cdot u=0&\text{in }\Omega\\
u=0&\text{on }\partial\Omega.\end{array}\right.

(B.26)

Recall from (
B.5
) that

u(x)=\int_{\Omega}G(x,y)\nabla\cdot F(y){\,{\rm d}y}=-\int_{\Omega}\nabla_{y}G(x,y)F(y){\,{\rm d}y}.

(B.27)

The energy estimate implies

\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}\leq C\int_{\Omega}|F|^{2}.

(B.28)

Next, we estimate
|u(x)|
and
|\nabla u(x)|
. Let
r=x_{3}
, which is comparable to
\delta(x)
since
x_{3}>2
. We consider two cases:
r<R/10
or
r>R/10
. If
r>R/10
,
since
F
is supported in
B_{R/2}(\hat{y})\cap\Omega
which does not intersect with
B_{R/10}(x)
, we can apply the interior Lipschitz estimate to
u
and (
B.28
)

|\nabla u(x)|\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{R/10}(x)}|\nabla u|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\leq CR^{-3/2}\bigg(\int_{\Omega}|F|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}.

(B.29)

On the other hand, we apply the interior estimate, Sobolev embedding and (
B.28
) to obtain

\displaystyle|u(x)|

\displaystyle\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{R/10}(x)}|u|^{6}\bigg)^{1/6}\leq CR^{-1/2}\bigg(\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}

(B.30)

\displaystyle\leq CR^{-1/2}\bigg(\int_{\Omega}|F|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}.

If
r<R/10
, by the interior and boundary Lipschitz estimate

\displaystyle|\nabla u(x)|

\displaystyle\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{r}(x)}|\nabla u|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{2r}(\hat{x})}|\nabla u|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}

(B.31)

\displaystyle\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{R/5}(\hat{x})}|\nabla u|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\leq CR^{-3/2}\bigg(\int_{\Omega}|F|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}.

Moreover, using the Poincaré inequality and the boundary Lipschitz estimate, we have

\displaystyle|u(x)|

\displaystyle\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{r}(x)}|u|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{2r}(\hat{x})}|u|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}

(B.32)

\displaystyle\leq Cr\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{2r}(\hat{x})}|\nabla u|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\leq Cr\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{R/5}(\hat{x})}|\nabla u|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq CrR^{-3/2}\bigg(\int_{\Omega}|F|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}.

From the estimates (
B.29
) - (
B.32
), (
B.27
) and duality, we see that

\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{R/2}(\hat{y})\cap\Omega}|\nabla_{x}\nabla_{y}G(x,z)|^{2}{\,{\rm d}z}\bigg)^{1/2}\leq\frac{C}{R^{3}},

(B.33)

and

\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{R/2}(\hat{y})\cap\Omega}|\nabla_{y}G(x,z)|^{2}{\,{\rm d}z}\bigg)^{1/2}\leq\frac{Cr}{R^{3}}.

(B.34)

Note that (
B.25
) and (
B.34
) combined lead to (
B.21
). To see (
B.22
), notice that
(\nabla_{x}G(x,y),\nabla_{x}\Pi(x,y))
is a weak solution in
y\in\Omega\setminus\{x\}
. Thus, we may apply (
B.33
), Poincaré inequality and boundary Lipschitz estimate to obtain

\displaystyle\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{1}(y)}|\nabla_{x}G(x,z)|^{2}\,{\rm d}z\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{{B_{3}(\hat{y})}}|\nabla_{x}G(x,z)|^{2}\,{\rm d}z\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{{B_{3}(\hat{y})}}|\nabla_{y}\nabla_{x}G(x,z)|^{2}\,{\rm d}z\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{R/2}(\hat{y})}|\nabla_{y}\nabla_{x}G(x,z)|^{2}\,{\rm d}z\bigg)^{1/2}\leq\frac{C}{R^{3}}.

The proof of (ii) thus is complete.
∎

Analogously, we can also show the estimates for
G
itself. The proof is left to the reader.

Proposition B.4
.

Let
L\in(0,\infty)
and
\Omega
be a bumpy John domain with constant
L
according to Definition
1.2
. The velocity component
G(x,y)
satisfies:

(i)

For
x_{3}>2
and
{y_{3}}>2
,

|G(x,y)|\leq C\min\bigg\{\frac{1}{|x-y|},\frac{\delta(x)}{|x-y|^{2}},\frac{\delta(y)}{|x-y|^{2}},\frac{\delta(x){\delta(y)}}{|x-y|^{3}}\bigg\}.

(B.35)

(ii)

For
x_{3}>2
and
|x-y|>{32}
,

\bigg(\int_{B_{1}(y)}|G(x,z)|^{2}\,{\rm d}z\bigg)^{1/2}\leq C\min\bigg\{\frac{1}{|x-y|},\frac{\delta(x)}{|x-y|^{2}},\frac{\delta(y)+1}{|x-y|^{2}},\frac{\delta(x)(\delta(y)+1)}{|x-y|^{3}}\bigg\}.

(B.36)

(iii)

For
y_{3}>2
and
|x-y|>{32}
,

\bigg(\int_{B_{1}(x)}|G(z,y)|^{2}\,{\rm d}z\bigg)^{1/2}\leq C\min\bigg\{\frac{1}{|x-y|},\frac{\delta(y)}{|x-y|^{2}},\frac{\delta(x)+1}{|x-y|^{2}},\frac{\delta(y)(\delta(x)+1)}{|x-y|^{3}}\bigg\}.

(B.37)

Here
C
depends on
L
.

B.3.
Large-scale estimates of the pressure component

The estimates of
\Pi
are stated as follows.

Proposition B.5
.

Let
L\in(0,\infty)
and
\Omega
be a bumpy John domain with constant
L
according to Definition
1.2
. The pressure component
\Pi(x,y)
satisfies
:

(i)

For
x_{3}>2
and
y_{3}>2
,

|\Pi(x,y)|\leq C\min\bigg\{\frac{1}{|x-y|^{2}},\frac{\delta(y)}{|x-y|^{3}}\bigg\}.

(B.38)

(ii)

For
x_{3}<2
and
y_{3}>2
with
|x-y|>{32}
,

\bigg(\int_{B_{1}(x)}|\Pi(z,y)|^{2}\,{\rm d}z\bigg)^{1/2}\leq C\min\bigg\{\frac{1}{|x-y|^{2}},\frac{\delta(y)}{|x-y|^{3}}\bigg\}.

(B.39)

(iii)

For
x_{3}>2
and
y_{3}<2
with
|x-y|>{32}
,

\bigg(\int_{B_{1}(y)}|\Pi(x,z)|^{2}\,{\rm d}z\bigg)^{1/2}\leq\frac{C}{|x-y|^{3}}.

(B.40)

Here
C
depends on
L
.

Proof.

We will carry out a delicate oscillation estimate of the pressure originating from
[
50
]
. We first consider the estimate (i), i.e.,
x_{3}>2
and
y_{3}>2
. Consider a point
w\in\Omega
with
w\neq y
. Let
t=|w-y|_{\infty}
.
We claim

\osc_{B_{t/4}(w)\cap\Omega_{>2}}\Pi(\cdot,y)\leq C\min\{\frac{1}{t^{2}},\frac{\delta(y)}{t^{3}}\}

(B.41)

with
C
independent of
t,w
, and
y
. The operator
\osc
is defined in (
B.7
).

We prove the above claim by considering different situations.
If
w\in B_{y_{3}/2}(y)
, then
t<\frac{y_{3}}{2}
and
B_{t}(w)\subset\Omega
. By the interior pressure estimate (
B.8
) in Lemma
B.1
and (
B.19
),

\displaystyle\osc_{B_{t/4}(w)}\Pi(\cdot,y)

\displaystyle\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{t/2}(w)}|\nabla_{x}G(z,y)|^{2}\,{\rm d}z\bigg)^{1/2}\leq C\min\{\frac{1}{t^{2}},\frac{\delta(y)}{t^{3}}\}.

Next, if
w\notin B_{y_{3}/2}(y)
, we consider two subcases: (a)
|w_{3}|<\frac{t}{4}
; (b)
|w_{3}|\geq\frac{t}{4}
. Without loss of generality, we assume
t>\frac{y_{3}}{2}>20
.

For the case (a), let
\hat{w}
be the projection of
w
on
\partial\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}
. Using the interior and boundary pressure estimates in John domains from Lemma
B.1
combined with a covering argument,

\osc_{B_{t/4}(w)\cap\Omega_{>2}}\Pi(\cdot,y)\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{t/2}(\hat{w})}|\nabla_{x}G(z,y)|^{2}\,{\rm d}z\bigg)^{1/2}\leq C\min\{\frac{1}{t^{2}},\frac{\delta(y)}{t^{3}}\},

(B.42)

where we have also used (
B.19
) and (
B.23
) in the second inequality.

Now, for the case (b),
B_{t/4}(w)
may be decomposed as a union of a finite number of cubes
B_{t/16}(w_{i})
with
i=1,2,\cdots,K_{0}
where
K_{0}
is an absolute constant, so that
B_{t/8}(w_{i})
is contained in
\Omega_{>2}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\osc_{B_{t/4}(w)}\Pi(\cdot,y)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{K_{0}}\osc_{B_{t/16}(w_{i})}\Pi(\cdot,y)

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{i=1}^{K_{0}}\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{t/8}(w_{i})}|\nabla_{x}G(z,y)|^{2}\,{\rm d}z\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq C\min\{\frac{1}{t^{2}},\frac{\delta(y)}{t^{3}}\},

where we have used (
B.19
).
Thus, the claim (
B.41
) is proved.

Now, by a covering argument, it is not difficult to see from (
B.41
) that, for any
r>0
,

\osc_{\Omega_{>2}\cap B_{2r}(y)\setminus\overline{B_{r}(y)}}\Pi(\cdot,y)=\osc_{(B_{2r}(y)\cap\Omega_{>2})\setminus\overline{(B_{r}(y)\cap\Omega_{>2})}}\Pi(\cdot,y)\leq C\min\{\frac{1}{r^{2}},\frac{\delta(y)}{r^{3}}\}.

Consequently,

\osc_{\Omega_{>2}\setminus\overline{B_{r}(y)}}\Pi(\cdot,y)\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\osc_{\Omega_{>2}\cap B_{2^{k}r}(y)\setminus\overline{B_{2^{k-1}r}(y)}}\leq C\min\{\frac{1}{r^{2}},\frac{\delta(y)}{r^{3}}\}.

(B.43)

This means that for each
y
with
y_{3}>2
,
there exists a function
\widehat{\Pi}(y)
such that

\lim_{|x|\to\infty,\,x_{3}>2}\Pi(x,y)=\widehat{\Pi}(y).

This convergence is uniform on any compact set in
\{y_{3}>2\}
.
We show that
\widehat{\Pi}(y)\equiv 0
. In fact, if
f\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\Omega)^{3}
,
the pressure of the Stokes equations with the source
f

is given by

p(x)=\int_{\Omega}\Pi(x,y)\cdot f(y)\,{\rm d}y.

By the definition of the Green function,
p(x)\to 0
holds as
|x_{3}|\to\infty
. It follows that

\int_{\Omega}\widehat{\Pi}(y)\cdot f(y)\,{\rm d}y=0.

This holds for any

f\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\Omega_{0})^{3}
where
\Omega_{0}
is a bounded open set whose closure is contained in
\{y_{3}>2\}
. Thus we have
\widehat{\Pi}(y)\equiv 0
.
Therefore, (
B.43
) implies (
B.38
)
since
r
is arbitrary.

Next, we prove (ii). Let
x_{3}<2,y_{3}>2
, and
r:=|x-y|_{\infty}
.
Without loss of generality, it suffices to assume
r>32
.
For such
x=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
, we pick
\tilde{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},3)
. Because
-1<x_{3}<2
and
{|x-\tilde{x}|_{\infty}}<4
, then
r-4\leq{|\tilde{x}-y|_{\infty}}\leq r+4
and hence by (i),

|\Pi(\tilde{x},y)|\leq C\min\bigg\{\frac{1}{(r-4)^{2}},\frac{\delta(y)}{(r-4)^{3}}\bigg\}\leq C\min\bigg\{\frac{1}{r^{2}},\frac{\delta(y)}{r^{3}}\bigg\}.

(B.44)

Next, we consider

|\Pi(\tilde{x},y)-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{3}(\hat{x})}\Pi(\cdot,y)|,

where
\hat{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},0)
is the projection
and
\Omega_{3}(\hat{x})
is the John domain between
\Omega\cap B_{3}(\hat{x})
and
\Omega\cap B_{6}(\hat{x})
given by Definition
1.2
. Following the argument
in the proof
of Lemma
B.1
, we can show

|\Pi(\tilde{x},y)-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{3}(\hat{x})}\Pi(\cdot,y)|\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{10}(\hat{x})}|\nabla_{x}G(z,y)|^{2}\,{\rm d}z\bigg)^{1/2}\leq C\min\bigg\{\frac{1}{r^{2}},\frac{\delta(y)}{r^{3}}\bigg\},

where we have used (
B.23
)
as well as (
B.19
) combined with a covering argument
and the fact

\text{dist}(\Omega_{10}(\hat{x}),y)\approx r

in the last inequality. On the other hand, observe that
\Omega\cap B_{1}(x)\subset\Omega_{3}(\hat{x})
. Hence, by the Bogovskii lemma in
\Omega_{3}(\hat{x})
and (
B.23
) with a covering argument,

\displaystyle\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{1}(x)}|\Pi(z,y)-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{3}(\hat{x})}\Pi(\cdot,y)|^{2}\,{\rm d}z\bigg)^{1/2}

(B.45)

\displaystyle\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{3}(\hat{x})}|\Pi(z,y)-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{3}(\hat{x})}\Pi(\cdot,y)|^{2}\,{\rm d}z\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq C\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{3}(\hat{x})}|\nabla_{x}G(z,y)|^{2}\,{\rm d}z\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq C\min\bigg\{\frac{1}{r^{2}},\frac{\delta(y)}{r^{3}}\bigg\}.

Combining the estimates above, we obtain

\bigg(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{1}(x)}|\Pi(z,y)|^{2}\,{\rm d}z\bigg)^{1/2}\leq C\min\bigg\{\frac{1}{r^{2}},\frac{\delta(y)}{r^{3}}\bigg\}.

This proves (
B.39
).

Next, we use a duality method to prove (iii). Let
f\in C_{0}^{\infty}(B_{1}(y)\cap\Omega)^{3}
, zero-extended to
\Omega
,
and consider

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\Delta u+\nabla p=f\chi_{B_{1}(y)}&\text{in }\Omega\\
\nabla\cdot u=0&\text{in }\Omega\\
u=0&\text{on }\partial\Omega.\end{array}\right.

(B.46)

By definition, the solution
(u,p)
with finite energy can be represented by (
B.5
).
Since we already know the estimate of
\nabla_{x}G
(namely, (
B.22
)),
we have

|\nabla u(x)|\leq\frac{C}{|x-y|^{3}}\|f\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(y))}

for
|x-y|_{\infty}>4
with
x_{3}>2
.
By a familiar oscillation argument, we obtain

|p(x)|=\bigg|\int_{\Omega}\Pi(x,y)\cdot f(y)\,{\rm d}y\bigg|\leq\frac{C}{|x-y|^{3}}\|f\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(y))}.

(B.47)

This implies (
B.40
).
∎

Appendix C
Proof of the iteration lemma

Proof of Lemma
3.10
.

The proof is a variation of the one in
[
84
]
. For fixed
r\in(\varepsilon,{\frac{1}{16}})
, the assumption (
3.37f
) implies

\displaystyle\int_{r}^{{1/8}}\frac{h(t)}{t}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{r}^{{1/8}}\frac{h(2t)}{t}\,{\rm d}t+C_{0}\int_{r}^{{1/8}}\frac{H(2t)}{t}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{2r}^{{1/4}}\frac{h(t)}{t}\,{\rm d}t+C_{0}\int_{2r}^{{1/4}}\frac{H(t)}{t}\,{\rm d}t,

which, combined with (
3.37b
), (
3.37d
) and (
3.37c
), gives

\displaystyle\int_{r}^{2r}\frac{h(t)}{t}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{{1/8}}^{{1/4}}\frac{h(t)}{t}\,{\rm d}t+C_{0}\int_{2r}^{{1/4}}\frac{H(t)}{t}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C{(\Phi(\frac{1}{2})+B_{0})}+C_{0}\int_{r}^{{1/2}}\frac{H(t)}{t}\,{\rm d}t.

Then from (
3.37f
) we have

\displaystyle\int_{r}^{2r}\frac{h(t)}{t}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\geq\int_{r}^{2r}\frac{h(r)-C_{0}H(2t)}{t}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\geq\frac{h(r)}{{4}}-C_{0}\int_{r}^{{1/2}}\frac{H(t)}{t}\,{\rm d}t.

Therefore for
r\in(\varepsilon,{\frac{1}{16}})
, we find

\displaystyle h(r)\leq C{(\Phi(\frac{1}{2})+B_{0})}+C\int_{r}^{{1/2}}\frac{H(t)}{t}\,{\rm d}t.

(C.1)

Let
\delta\in(0,\min\{\frac{\theta}{4},{\frac{1}{(16)^{2}}}\})
be a small number to be determined later and let us set
\varepsilon_{*}=\delta^{2}
. We temporarily assume that
\varepsilon\in(0,\theta\varepsilon_{*})
in the following proof. From (
3.37a
) we have

\displaystyle\int_{\varepsilon/\delta}^{\delta}\frac{H(\theta t)}{t}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\varepsilon/\delta}^{\delta}\frac{H({2t})}{t}\,{\rm d}t+C_{0}\bigg(\int_{\varepsilon/\delta}^{\delta}\big(\frac{\varepsilon}{t}\big)^{\alpha}\frac{\Phi({16}t)}{t}\,{\rm d}t+B_{0}\int_{\varepsilon/\delta}^{\delta}t^{\beta-1}\,{\rm d}t\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\varepsilon/\delta}^{{1/2}}\frac{H(t)}{t}\,{\rm d}t+C_{0}\bigg(\int_{\varepsilon/\delta}^{\delta}\big(\frac{\varepsilon}{t}\big)^{\alpha}\frac{\Phi({16}t)}{t}\,{\rm d}t+\beta^{-1}B_{0}\bigg).

From (
3.37e
) and the estimate (
C.1
) for
h(r)
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\varepsilon/\delta}^{\delta}\big(\frac{\varepsilon}{t}\big)^{\alpha}\frac{\Phi({16}t)}{t}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}\int_{\varepsilon/\delta}^{\delta}\big(\frac{\varepsilon}{t}\big)^{\alpha}\frac{H({16}t)+h({16}t)}{t}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}\delta^{\alpha}\int_{{16}\varepsilon/\delta}^{{16}\delta}\frac{H(t)}{t}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+C\bigg(\int_{\varepsilon/\delta}^{\delta}\big(\frac{\varepsilon}{t}\big)^{\alpha}\frac{\,{\rm d}t}{t}\bigg)\bigg({(\Phi(\frac{1}{2})+B_{0})}+\int_{{16}\varepsilon/\delta}^{{1/2}}\frac{H(t)}{t}\,{\rm d}t\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq(C_{0}+C_{1}\alpha^{-1})\delta^{\alpha}\int_{\varepsilon/\delta}^{{1/2}}\frac{H(t)}{t}\,{\rm d}t+C_{1}\alpha^{-1}\delta^{\alpha}{(\Phi(\frac{1}{2})+B_{0})}.

Now let us choose
\delta
sufficiently small depending on
\alpha
,
C_{0}
and
C_{1}
so that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}+C_{0}(C_{0}+C_{1}\alpha^{-1})\delta^{\alpha}\leq\frac{3}{4}.

Then we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\theta\varepsilon/\delta}^{\theta\delta}\frac{H(t)}{t}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{4}\int_{\varepsilon/\delta}^{{1/2}}\frac{H(t)}{t}\,{\rm d}t+C{(\Phi(\frac{1}{2})+B_{0})},

and consequently, from
\varepsilon/\delta<\theta\delta
,

\displaystyle\int_{\theta\varepsilon/\delta}^{\theta\delta}\frac{H(t)}{t}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq 3\int_{\theta\delta}^{{1/2}}\frac{H(t)}{t}\,{\rm d}t+C{(\Phi(\frac{1}{2})+B_{0})}.

Therefore from (
3.37b
) and (
3.37c
) we have

\displaystyle\int_{\theta\varepsilon/\delta}^{{1/2}}\frac{H(t)}{t}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq 4\int_{\theta\delta}^{{1/2}}\frac{H(t)}{t}\,{\rm d}t+C{(\Phi(\frac{1}{2})+B_{0})}

(C.2)

\displaystyle\leq C{(\Phi(\frac{1}{2})+B_{0})},

where we have used

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\delta\leq r\leq 1/2}\Phi(r)\leq C{(\Phi(\frac{1}{2})+B_{0})}

(C.3)

with some constant
C
independent of
\varepsilon
, which is proved by applying (
3.37c
) finitely many times. Hence, from
4\varepsilon<\theta\varepsilon/\delta
, the estimates (
C.1
) and (
C.2
) lead to, for
r\in(\theta\varepsilon/\delta,{\frac{1}{16}})
,

\displaystyle h(r)

\displaystyle\leq C{(\Phi(\frac{1}{2})+B_{0})}+C\int_{\theta\varepsilon/\delta}^{{1/2}}\frac{H(t)}{t}\,{\rm d}t

(C.4)

\displaystyle\leq C{(\Phi(\frac{1}{2})+B_{0})}.

For
r\in(\theta\varepsilon/\delta,{\frac{1}{32}})
, from (
3.37e
), (
C.2
) and (
C.4
), we see that

\displaystyle\int_{r}^{2r}\frac{\Phi(t)}{t}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}\int_{r}^{2r}\frac{H(t)}{t}\,{\rm d}t+C_{0}\int_{r}^{2r}\frac{h(t)}{t}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C{(\Phi(\frac{1}{2})+B_{0})}.

From this, using the following inequality valid for all fixed
r\in({2\varepsilon},1/2)

\displaystyle\Phi(r)

\displaystyle\leq C\big(\Phi(t)+B_{0}t^{\beta}\big),\hskip 10.00002ptt\in[r,2r],

which is a consequence of (
3.37c
), we find

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\varepsilon/\delta\leq r\leq 1/{32}}\Phi(r)\leq C{(\Phi(\frac{1}{2})+B_{0})}.

Using repeatedly (
3.37c
) finitely many times, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{\varepsilon\leq r\leq 1/{32}}\Phi(r)\leq C{(\Phi(\frac{1}{2})+B_{0})}

(C.5)

with a constant
C
independent of
\varepsilon
. On the other hand, (
3.37b
) and (
C.5
) imply

\displaystyle\int_{\varepsilon}^{\theta\varepsilon/\delta}\frac{H(t)}{t}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}\int_{\varepsilon}^{\theta\varepsilon/\delta}\frac{\Phi(t)}{t}\,{\rm d}t

(C.6)

\displaystyle\leq C{(\Phi(\frac{1}{2})+B_{0})}.

Combining (
C.2
), (
C.3
), (
C.5
) and (
C.6
), we obtain the assertion (
3.38
), provided
\varepsilon\in(0,\theta\varepsilon_{*})
. Finally, if
\varepsilon\in(\theta\varepsilon_{*},{\frac{1}{48}})
, (
3.38
) is trivial by applying (
3.37b
) and (
3.37c
) finitely many times.
∎
